Real HR Simulation
Sandbox Evaluation Workbook
Version 1.0
Prepared by Dr. James W. Cooper, Jr.
Structured Improvisation Learning Technologies AI (SILT-AI)



Purpose
Thank you for participating in the evaluation of the Real HR Simulation Sandbox. This workbook
is designed to document your experiences while interacting with the simulation. We are
evaluating software functionality, usability, learning effectiveness, HR domain realism, and the
overall educational experience. Please record any observations that come to mind.



Tester Information
•   Date: ___August 1, 2026_______________________
•   Name (Optional): _____James Cooper_____________________
•   Background: Undergraduate / Graduate / HR Professional / Faculty / Other
•   HR Experience: None / Beginner / Intermediate / Advanced
•   Simulation Version: _____Iteration 4_____________________
•   Browser: Chrome / Edge / Firefox / Safari / Other




Overall First Impressions – Landing Page
    •   The landing page is visually clean, professional, and easy to understand. The
        current structure has the appearance and usability of an established higher-
        education business simulation platform. For future iterations, we should begin
        considering opportunities to create a more distinctive Real HR Simulation visual
        identity; however, that is not a priority for Iteration #4.
    •   Retain the four visual information cards currently displayed in the middle of the
        page. These provide an effective overview of the simulation's major
        characteristics.
    •   Replace the heading “Seven HR decision modules” above the four cards with
        “Why Real HR Simulation?” The four cards explain the value and structure of
        the simulation rather than the individual decision modules.

                                        Page 1 of 420
   •   Move “Seven HR Decision Modules” below the four information cards and
       immediately above the seven HR module selections.
   •   Capitalize Decision Modules consistently in the section heading.
   •   Convert each of the seven module names into an active link/card: Recruitment &
       Selection, Performance Management, Training & Development, Employee
       Relations, Compensation & Benefits, Org Design & Change, and DEI Initiatives.
   •   Each module link should open a simple pre-simulation informational page
       describing: (1) what the HR area is, (2) why it matters to organizational
       performance, (3) what types of decisions students will encounter, and (4) how
       the area connects to broader Real HR decision-making and organizational
       outcomes.
   •   These pages should provide foreshadowing and instructional scaffolding
       without revealing scoring formulas, optimal decisions, or simulation answers.
   •   Include navigation allowing students to return to the main Real HR Simulation
       landing page and/or proceed to Sign In.
   •   Learning Design Purpose: Transform the landing page from primarily a
       product/login page into the first stage of the simulation's learning experience.
       Students should begin developing an understanding of the HR decision
       environment before entering the actual simulation.

Note for Amit:

Iteration #4 Boundary:
Do not overbuild these module pages. For this iteration, they can be lightweight
informational pages using the existing simulation visual design. Their purpose is
orientation and learning readiness. More sophisticated multimedia, examples, videos, HR
Coach integration, and instructional resources can be considered in later iterations.

That last boundary is important. You have a lot more structural work to validate in
Iteration #4, so I would add the instructional architecture now without allowing the
landing-page enhancements to consume development time needed for the engine,
professor/admin environment, and testing infrastructure.

Seven HR Decision Modules (Pre-
simulation learning buttons)

Recruitment & Selection Pre-Simulation
Page – Design & Programming Notes
  •   Create a dedicated Recruitment & Selection pre-simulation information page
      accessible by clicking the Recruitment & Selection card from the Real HR
      Simulation public landing page.
  •   This page is not the Recruitment decision-making screen used during an active
      simulation round. It is an instructional orientation page available before students
      enter the simulation.
  •   Use the approved Recruitment & Selection image as the visual design reference.
  •   Maintain Amit's existing Real HR Simulation design language: white background,
      dark navy text, orange primary accent, clean cards, restrained secondary colors,
      consistent icons, and generous spacing.
  •   Maintain the standard top navigation: Real HR Simulation | Simulator | Sign In
      | Register.
  •   Include “← Back to Real HR Simulation” near the top so users can return to the
      main landing page.
  •   The page should be accessible without requiring students to log in. Its purpose
      is to introduce prospective/current students to the decision environment before
      simulation participation.

Page Introduction

  •   Display the primary heading “Recruitment & Selection.”
  •   Display the subtitle “Building the Workforce Your Organization Needs.”
  •   Use the Recruitment & Selection icon from the main landing page to reinforce
      visual continuity.
  •   Include a professional recruitment/hiring image on the right side of the
      introductory section.
  •   Use the introductory paragraph to establish the basic learning context:
      Recruitment & Selection involves identifying workforce needs, attracting
      candidates, evaluating talent, and making hiring decisions that support
      organizational goals.
  •   Emphasize that recruitment in the Real HR Simulation is not simply filling
      positions. Students must consider workforce capability, organizational
      performance, strategy, and available resources.

                                    Page 4 of 420
1. What Is Recruitment & Selection?

  •   Create the first instructional section titled “1. What Is Recruitment &
      Selection?”
  •   Explain the distinction between attracting potential employees and selecting
      candidates who meet organizational needs.
  •   Introduce the idea that HR professionals balance multiple considerations,
      including:
          o Cost
          o Speed
          o Candidate quality
          o Workforce demand
          o Organizational strategy
  •   Keep this section conceptual. Do not display simulation formulas, weights,
      scoring relationships, or preferred decision combinations.
  •   Reinforce that Recruitment & Selection operates within a broader organizational
      system rather than as an isolated HR activity.

2. Why Does It Matter?

  •   Create a four-card section titled “2. Why Does It Matter?”
  •   Use four horizontally aligned visual cards:
          o Talent Availability
          o Workforce Quality
          o Employee Experience
          o Organizational Performance
  •   Give each card a simple icon and restrained accent color.
  •   Talent Availability: explain that recruitment helps determine whether the
      organization has sufficient employees and capabilities to perform its work.
  •   Workforce Quality: explain that hiring decisions influence the knowledge, skills,
      capabilities, and potential entering the organization.
  •   Employee Experience: explain that recruitment and onboarding create some of
      the employee's earliest organizational experiences.
  •   Organizational Performance: explain that talent decisions can influence
      workforce capacity, productivity, costs, retention, and business performance.
  •   Keep each explanation brief enough that students can scan the four concepts
      quickly.

3. Decisions You May Encounter

  •   Create a section titled “3. Decisions You May Encounter.”
  •   Use the phrase “may encounter” intentionally. The page should prepare
      students for the nature of the decision environment without revealing exact round
      configurations.
  •   Initial concepts may include:
          o Workforce hiring needs


                                    Page 5 of 420
          o   Recruitment spending and resource allocation
          o   Recruiting channels and approaches
          o   Time and resources devoted to filling positions
          o   Candidate quality and selection priorities
          o   Onboarding investments
          o   Staffing levels and workforce capacity
          o   Tradeoffs between recruitment and other HR investments
   •   These are decision categories, not recommended decisions.
   •   Do not show default values, optimal spending levels, scoring thresholds, or
       formulas.

Think Like an HR Leader

   •   Include the orange-accented “Think Like an HR Leader” callout directly
       underneath the decision concepts.
   •   Use this callout to introduce the idea of tradeoffs.
   •   Explain that faster hiring may require additional resources and that greater
       investment does not automatically produce better outcomes.
   •   Reinforce that students should consider strategy, workforce conditions,
       organizational needs, and available resources when making decisions.
   •   This should encourage managerial judgment rather than tell students what
       decision to make.

4. Connecting Recruitment to the Organization

   •   Create a section titled “4. Connecting Recruitment to the Organization.”
   •   Explain that Recruitment & Selection decisions may interact with Compensation
       & Benefits, Training & Development, Performance Management, Employee
       Relations, DEI Initiatives, and other areas of the simulation.
   •   Include the three-stage visual pathway shown in the approved design:

Recruitment Decisions → Workforce Outcomes → Organizational Outcomes

   •   Under Recruitment Decisions, use examples such as:
          o Hiring
          o Recruiting Investment
          o Selection
          o Onboarding
   •   Under Workforce Outcomes, use:
          o Staffing
          o Hiring Quality
          o Employee Experience
          o Retention
          o Capability
   •   Under Organizational Outcomes, use:
          o Productivity


                                      Page 6 of 420
          o    Costs
          o    Financial Performance
          o    Strategic Performance
   •   Use arrows between the three cards to visually communicate the relationship.
   •   This visual is conceptual rather than mathematical. It should show students
       that decisions can have downstream consequences without revealing the
       simulation engine's actual formulas.
   •   End this section with the statement: “The challenge is to manage the workforce
       as an interconnected system.”

Before You Enter the Simulation

   •   Create a final instructional section titled “Before You Enter the Simulation.”
   •   Include three reflection questions:
           1. What talent does our organization need to execute its strategy?
           2. What tradeoffs might we face between hiring speed, cost, and
              workforce quality?
           3. How could today's recruitment decisions affect the organization in
              future rounds?
   •   These questions are for learning readiness and reflection only.
   •   Students do not need to submit answers on this page during Iteration #4.
   •   We can later consider whether these questions become part of orientation, Team
       Reflection, instructor discussion, or other learning activities.

Bottom Navigation

   •   Include two prominent navigation buttons at the bottom:
           o Explore Other HR Decision Modules
           o Sign In to Begin
   •   Explore Other HR Decision Modules should return students to the Decision
       Module area of the main landing page or eventually open a module-selection
       interface.
   •   Sign In to Begin should direct users to the existing simulation login.
   •   Maintain Amit's orange primary action color for Sign In to Begin.

Instructional Guardrails

   •   Do not expose scoring formulas.
   •   Do not expose decision weights.
   •   Do not identify optimal decisions.
   •   Do not provide recommended dollar amounts.
   •   Do not reveal how specific decisions mathematically change particular
       metrics.
   •   Do not tell students what choices will maximize their HR Balance Scorecard.



                                    Page 7 of 420
   •   Students should understand what variables and relationships deserve
       consideration, but they should still have to discover consequences through
       decision-making, results, feedback, reflection, and subsequent rounds.

Reusable Architecture for All Seven Modules

   •   Recruitment & Selection should establish the standard page architecture for the
       other six Decision Modules.
   •   Once this page is implemented correctly, reuse the structure for:
           o Performance Management
           o Training & Development
           o Employee Relations
           o Compensation & Benefits
           o Org Design & Change
           o DEI Initiatives
   •   Every page should follow the same learning sequence:

Module Introduction → What Is It? → Why Does It Matter? → Decisions You May
Encounter → Think Like an HR Leader → Connection to Organizational Outcomes
→ Before You Enter the Simulation

   •   Content, icons, images, examples, and conceptual relationships will change by
       module, but the instructional architecture and navigation should remain
       consistent.

Iteration #4 Boundary

   •   Do not overbuild these pages during Iteration #4. They should initially be
       lightweight, responsive informational pages.
   •   We do not currently need videos, animations, quizzes, embedded HR Coach
       functionality, extensive readings, or multimedia learning objects.
   •   The priority is to establish the page architecture, navigation, content structure,
       and instructional scaffolding.
   •   More sophisticated learning resources can be layered into the pages during future
       iterations.

Important Design Principle for Amit: These pages are not marketing pages and they
are not simulation decision pages. They represent the first instructional layer of the
Real HR Simulation experience. Their purpose is to give students enough domain
knowledge and conceptual scaffolding to enter the simulation prepared to make
thoughtful decisions—while preserving the uncertainty, tradeoffs, and discovery
necessary for experiential learning.

Overall learning objective: The student should leave the Recruitment & Selection
preview understanding what the HR function does, why it matters, what kinds of
managerial decisions it involves, and how those decisions can ripple through the

                                     Page 8 of 420
workforce and organization—without knowing what the “right” simulation decision
will be.

Performance Management Pre-
Simulation Page – Design &
Programming Notes
   •   Create a dedicated Performance Management pre-simulation learning page
       accessible by clicking the Performance Management Decision Module card on the
       Real HR Simulation landing page.

   •   This is an orientation and instructional scaffolding page, not the actual
       Performance Management decision screen students will use during simulation
       rounds.

   •   Use the approved Performance Management image as the visual design reference.

   •   Maintain the standardized Real HR Simulation design established with Recruitment
       & Selection: white background, navy headings, orange primary accents, restrained
       secondary colors, clean cards, icons, consistent spacing, and professional imagery.

   •   Maintain the public top navigation: Real HR Simulation | Simulator | Sign In |
       Register.

   •   Include “← Back to Real HR Simulation” at the top of the page.

   •   This page should be accessible before login so students can explore the HR
       Decision Modules before entering the simulation.

Page Introduction

   •   Display the primary heading “Performance Management.”

   •   Use the subtitle “Driving Performance, Development, and Results.”

   •   Use the Performance Management checklist icon consistent with the module icon on
       the main landing page.

   •   Include professional imagery representing a manager/employee performance
       conversation, coaching discussion, or performance review.

   •   The opening explanation should introduce Performance Management as the process
       of setting expectations, providing feedback, evaluating results, and supporting
       employee growth.



                                     Page 11 of 420
   •   Reinforce that Performance Management decisions can influence employee
       motivation, capability, retention, productivity, and organizational outcomes.

   •   Establish from the beginning that Performance Management is broader than
       conducting an annual performance appraisal.

1. What Is Performance Management?

   •   Create the first instructional section titled “1. What Is Performance
       Management?”

   •   Explain that Performance Management helps employees understand what is
       expected, provides ongoing feedback, and evaluates contributions and
       competencies.

   •   Introduce the major conceptual activities:

           o   Goal setting

           o   Performance monitoring

           o   Feedback

           o   Evaluation

           o   Recognition

           o   Development planning

   •   Explain that students will eventually make decisions affecting how employees are
       evaluated, developed, supported, and recognized.

   •   Keep the discussion conceptual and introductory.

   •   Do not display formulas, weights, thresholds, score calculations, or optimal
       Performance Management configurations.

2. Why Does It Matter?

   •   Create a four-card section titled “2. Why Does It Matter?”

   •   Retain the four-card visual architecture used on the Recruitment & Selection page.

   •   The four Performance Management cards should be:

Aligns Effort with Strategy

   •   Helps employees focus their work on activities supporting organizational priorities
       and goals.


                                      Page 12 of 420
Improves Performance and Productivity

   •   Clear expectations, feedback, and coaching can help employees improve and
       contribute more effectively.

Supports Employee Growth and Retention

   •   Feedback and development opportunities can influence engagement, satisfaction,
       capability, and retention.

Drives Organizational Results

   •   Strong Performance Management practices can influence quality, productivity,
       efficiency, costs, and financial outcomes.

   •   Give each card its own simple icon and restrained accent color.

   •   Keep descriptions concise and scannable.

3. Decisions You May Encounter

   •   Create the section “3. Decisions You May Encounter.”

   •   Continue using “may encounter” so students receive foreshadowing without
       knowing exactly what will appear during a particular round.

   •   Potential Performance Management decision areas include:

          o   Performance expectations and goal setting

          o   Performance evaluation criteria and standards

          o   Feedback frequency and quality

          o   Performance review process design

          o   Recognition and rewards for high performance

          o   Corrective action and performance improvement support

          o   Development plans and career growth opportunities

          o   Allocation of time and resources to Performance Management programs

   •   These should be presented as decision categories rather than recommended
       actions.

   •   Do not provide suggested spending levels, review frequencies, thresholds, or “best”
       approaches.



                                     Page 13 of 420
Think Like an HR Leader

   •   Retain the orange-accented “Think Like an HR Leader” callout.

   •   Reinforce that Performance Management is about more than evaluation.

   •   Ask students to consider how Performance Management decisions could influence:

          o   Employee motivation

          o   Behavior

          o   Skill development

          o   Accountability

          o   Long-term organizational performance

   •   Reinforce that choices involve tradeoffs and should align with organizational
       strategy and available resources.

   •   Avoid language suggesting that one particular Performance Management approach
       will automatically produce superior simulation results.

4. Connecting Performance Management to the Organization

   •   Create the section “4. Connecting Performance Management to the
       Organization.”

   •   Explain that Performance Management decisions interact with other HR areas and
       influence outcomes throughout the organization.

   •   Retain the same three-stage visual framework established on the Recruitment page:

Performance Management Decisions → Workforce Outcomes → Organizational
Outcomes

Under Performance Management Decisions, display:

   •   Goal Setting

   •   Evaluations

   •   Feedback

   •   Recognition

   •   Development Support

Under Workforce Outcomes, display:


                                     Page 14 of 420
   •   Employee Performance

   •   Engagement

   •   Skills and Capability

   •   Motivation

   •   Retention

Under Organizational Outcomes, display:

   •   Productivity

   •   Quality

   •   Costs

   •   Financial Performance

   •   Strategic Performance

   •   Use arrows between the three boxes to visually communicate downstream
       relationships.

   •   Keep this relationship conceptual rather than mathematical.

   •   Do not reveal which variables affect specific engine scores or the magnitude of those
       effects.

   •   End the section with the learning statement:

“The challenge is to create a performance culture that drives results while developing
people and sustaining engagement.”

Before You Enter the Simulation

   •   Maintain the standardized “Before You Enter the Simulation” section.

   •   Use three Performance Management reflection questions:

   1. What performance expectations will help our organization achieve its goals?

   2. How will we balance recognition, feedback, and development in our
      approach?

   3. How could our Performance Management decisions impact employees in
      future rounds?




                                      Page 15 of 420
   •   These questions are intended to activate prior knowledge and encourage students
       to begin thinking strategically before entering the simulation.

   •   No response submission is required in Iteration #4.

   •   The questions can later become part of Team Reflection, classroom discussion,
       professor facilitation, or other learning activities.

Bottom Navigation

   •   Retain the two standardized navigation buttons:

          o   Explore Other HR Decision Modules

          o   Sign In to Begin

   •   Explore Other HR Decision Modules should return students to the module-
       selection area.

   •   Sign In to Begin should take students to the simulation login page.

   •   Maintain Amit's orange primary-action color for Sign In to Begin.

Cross-Module Connections

   •   Performance Management should not be presented as an isolated HR function.

   •   The content should prepare students to recognize possible relationships with:

          o   Recruitment & Selection

          o   Training & Development

          o   Compensation & Benefits

          o   Employee Relations

          o   Org Design & Change

          o   DEI Initiatives

   •   For example, Performance Management may identify capability gaps that create
       Training & Development needs; recognition decisions may intersect with
       Compensation; poorly managed performance processes may influence Employee
       Relations.

   •   These connections should remain conceptual. Do not reveal the engine's exact
       causal weights or formulas.

Instructional Guardrails

                                     Page 16 of 420
   •   Do not show the Performance Management scoring formula.

   •   Do not show how Review Coverage or Productivity Index is calculated.

   •   Do not reveal specific metric weights.

   •   Do not provide an ideal review-coverage percentage.

   •   Do not provide optimal spending amounts.

   •   Do not identify which combination of decisions maximizes the HR Balance
       Scorecard.

   •   Do not provide advance feedback that students would normally receive after making
       simulation decisions.

   •   The page should teach students what to think about, not what to choose.

Consistency Across the Seven Pre-Simulation Pages

   •   Maintain the same learning architecture established with Recruitment & Selection:

Module Introduction → What Is It? → Why Does It Matter? → Decisions You May
Encounter → Think Like an HR Leader → Connection to Organizational Outcomes →
Before You Enter the Simulation

   •   Keep section placement, navigation, card structure, typography, and general page
       dimensions consistent across all seven pages.

   •   Change the domain content, imagery, icons, accent details, decisions,
       workforce outcomes, and reflection questions according to the HR function.

   •   This consistency should allow students to focus on learning the HR content rather
       than relearning the interface for every module.

Iteration #4 Boundary

   •   Keep this page lightweight for Iteration #4.

   •   We do not currently need videos, quizzes, embedded readings, HR Coach
       functionality, interactive cases, or detailed instructional exercises.

   •   The immediate purpose is to establish the Performance Management learning
       orientation and connect it to the broader simulation architecture.

   •   More advanced instructional resources can be layered into the module in future
       iterations.

Important Design Principle for Amit: The Performance Management pre-simulation page
should help students recognize that Performance Management is a continuous

                                      Page 17 of 420
organizational system of expectations, feedback, evaluation, recognition, and
development—not simply an annual performance review. At the same time, the page
must preserve the uncertainty of the simulation by avoiding formulas, optimal choices, or
advance knowledge of how specific decisions will score.

Overall learning objective: Before entering the simulation, students should understand
what Performance Management is, why organizations invest in it, the kinds of
managerial decisions it requires, and how those decisions may influence employees
and organizational performance over time.

Training & Development Pre-Simulation
Page – Design & Programming Notes
  •   Create a dedicated Training & Development pre-simulation learning page
      accessible by clicking the Training & Development card on the public Real HR
      Simulation landing page.
  •   This page is an instructional orientation page, not the actual Training &
      Development decision interface used during simulation rounds.
  •   Use the approved Training & Development image as the visual design reference.
  •   Maintain the same Real HR Simulation visual standards already established:
          o White background
          o Navy headings/text
          o Orange primary accents
          o Clean rounded cards
          o Restrained secondary accent colors
          o Consistent icons
          o Professional HR/workplace imagery
          o Consistent spacing and typography
  •   Maintain the public navigation: Real HR Simulation | Simulator | Sign In |
      Register.
  •   Include “← Back to Real HR Simulation” at the top of the page.
  •   The page should remain accessible before login so students can explore the HR
      decision areas before entering the simulation.

Page Introduction

  •   Display the primary heading “Training & Development.”
  •   Use the subtitle “Building Skills, Strengthening Capability, Driving Growth.”
  •   Use the Training & Development icon from the main landing page.
  •   Include professional imagery showing a workplace learning environment, training
      session, leadership development activity, or employee development setting.
  •   The introductory text should explain that Training & Development helps
      employees build the knowledge, skills, and capabilities required to perform
      today and prepare for future organizational needs.
  •   Reinforce that T&D decisions may influence:
          o Employee capability
          o Productivity
          o Innovation
          o Retention
          o Long-term organizational performance



                                  Page 20 of 420
   •   Establish that effective T&D requires organizations to have the right skills, at the
       right time, aligned with strategy and changing business needs.

1. What Is Training & Development?

   •   Create the first instructional section titled “1. What Is Training &
       Development?”
   •   Explain that Training & Development involves planned activities designed to
       improve employee skills, competencies, and performance.
   •   Introduce the broader scope of T&D, including:
           o Onboarding and foundational training
           o Technical training
           o Compliance training
           o Leadership development
           o Career development
   •   Explain that students may eventually make decisions about how to invest in
       learning and development to:
           o Address skill gaps
           o Improve performance
           o Build workforce capability
           o Support future organizational needs
   •   Keep this section conceptual.
   •   Do not disclose training formulas, scoring logic, thresholds, or optimal
       investment levels.

2. Why Does It Matter?

   •   Create a four-card section titled “2. Why Does It Matter?”
   •   Retain the same four-card structure used across the other pre-simulation learning
       pages.
   •   Use these four areas:

Enhances Skills and Capability

   •   Employees gain knowledge and skills needed to perform effectively in current
       and future roles.

Improves Performance and Innovation

   •   Well-designed training can support productivity, quality, innovation, and
       continuous improvement.

Supports Engagement and Retention

   •   Investment in employee growth can influence motivation, engagement,
       satisfaction, and the likelihood employees remain with the organization.

                                     Page 21 of 420
Drives Organizational Growth

   •   A capable workforce helps the organization adapt to change, compete effectively,
       and support strategic objectives.
   •   Use a simple icon for each card.
   •   Keep content concise enough to be scanned quickly.

3. Decisions You May Encounter

   •   Create the section “3. Decisions You May Encounter.”
   •   Continue using “may encounter” so the page provides orientation without
       revealing exact simulation configurations.
   •   Initial decision areas may include:
           o Identifying skill gaps and training needs
           o Allocating training budgets across programs
           o Choosing training types and delivery methods
           o Balancing mandatory/compliance training with developmental training
           o Investing in leadership and career development
           o Scheduling training and managing time away from work
           o Measuring training effectiveness
           o Balancing training investment against other HR priorities
   •   These should be presented as decision categories, not recommendations.
   •   Do not provide specific dollar amounts, recommended allocations, ideal training
       levels, or optimal combinations.

Think Like an HR Leader

   •   Retain the standardized orange-accented “Think Like an HR Leader” callout.
   •   Reinforce the idea that training is an investment, and every investment
       involves tradeoffs.
   •   Include the concept that more training is not automatically better.
   •   Students should consider:
           o Workforce needs
           o Expected returns
           o Strategic alignment
           o Available resources
           o Competing HR priorities
   •   The purpose is to encourage managerial judgment rather than tell students how
       much they should invest.

4. Connecting Training & Development to the Organization

   •   Create the section “4. Connecting Training & Development to the
       Organization.”
   •   Explain that T&D decisions interact with other HR functions and may influence
       outcomes across the organization.

                                    Page 22 of 420
   •   Retain the standardized three-stage visual architecture:

T&D Decisions → Workforce Outcomes → Organizational Outcomes

Under T&D Decisions, display:

   •   Needs Assessment
   •   Training Investment
   •   Program Selection
   •   Delivery Methods
   •   Development Support

Under Workforce Outcomes, display:

   •   Skills and Capability
   •   Employee Performance
   •   Engagement
   •   Adaptability
   •   Retention

Under Organizational Outcomes, display:

   •   Productivity
   •   Quality
   •   Innovation
   •   Costs
   •   Financial Performance
   •   Strategic Performance
   •   Use arrows between the three boxes.
   •   Keep this visual conceptual rather than mathematical.
   •   Do not show exact cause-and-effect weights or how the engine calculates
       Training ROI or Training Effectiveness.
   •   End this section with the statement:

“The challenge is to build a learning culture that strengthens capability today and
prepares your organization for future success.”

Before You Enter the Simulation

   •   Retain the standardized “Before You Enter the Simulation” section.
   •   Use these three Training & Development reflection questions:

   1. What skills and capabilities are most critical for our organization right now?
   2. How will we balance short-term performance needs with long-term
      development?


                                     Page 23 of 420
   3. How could our training decisions impact employees and results in future
      rounds?

   •   These prompts are intended to encourage reflection and activate prior knowledge.
   •   No submission is required during Iteration #4.
   •   These questions may later connect to Team Reflection, classroom debriefs, or
       professor facilitation.

Bottom Navigation

   •   Retain the two standardized buttons:
           o Explore Other HR Decision Modules
           o Sign In to Begin
   •   Explore Other HR Decision Modules should return users to the decision-module
       area.
   •   Sign In to Begin should take users to the simulation login page.
   •   Maintain Amit’s orange primary-action color.

Cross-Module Connections

   •   Make clear that Training & Development is connected to the broader HR system.
   •   Conceptually, T&D may interact with:
           o Recruitment & Selection
           o Performance Management
           o Compensation & Benefits
           o Employee Relations
           o Org Design & Change
           o DEI Initiatives
   •   For example:
           o Performance Management may reveal capability gaps that create training
              needs.
           o Recruitment decisions may affect the skills entering the organization.
           o Organizational change may create new learning requirements.
           o Career development may influence engagement and retention.
   •   Keep these relationships conceptual only.

Instructional Guardrails

   •   Do not show the Training ROI formula.
   •   Do not show the Training Effectiveness formula.
   •   Do not reveal ideal training investment levels.
   •   Do not disclose scoring weights.
   •   Do not identify which training choices maximize Learning & Growth scores.
   •   Do not reveal which combination of training decisions produces the highest
       simulation result.
   •   Do not provide advance feedback that belongs in the results phase.

                                    Page 24 of 420
   •   The page should tell students what to consider, not what to choose.

Consistency Across the Seven Pages

   •   Maintain the same standardized learning sequence:

Module Introduction → What Is It? → Why Does It Matter? → Decisions You May
Encounter → Think Like an HR Leader → Connection to Organizational Outcomes
→ Before You Enter the Simulation

          o Section order

   •   Change:
          o Domain content
          o Icons
          o Professional image
          o Decision categories
          o Workforce outcomes
          o Organizational outcomes

Iteration #4 Boundary

   •   Keep this page lightweight and informational during Iteration #4.
   •   We do not need videos, quizzes, embedded readings, AI coaching, or extensive
       interactive learning objects yet.
   •   The immediate goal is to establish the T&D orientation, consistent learning
       architecture, and pre-simulation instructional scaffolding.
   •   More advanced instructional content can be added later.

Important Design Principle for Amit: The Training & Development page should help
students understand that learning investment is a strategic workforce capability
decision, not simply a training expense. At the same time, it must preserve the discovery
process by avoiding formulas, optimal choices, or advance knowledge of exactly how
training decisions will affect simulation scores.

Overall learning objective: Before entering the simulation, students should understand
what Training & Development is, why workforce capability matters, the types of
decisions HR leaders must make, and how learning investments may influence
employee and organizational performance over time.

Important Content Boundary: Employee Relations vs. DEI

One distinction will be important as Amit builds these pages.

   •   Employee Relations may reference fairness, inclusion, and equitable treatment,
       but it should not absorb the content of the separate DEI Initiatives module.

   •   Employee Relations should primarily focus on employee-management
       relationships, workplace concerns, communication, conflict, policy, fairness,
       trust, and workplace climate.

   •   The DEI page can later address the broader strategic issues associated with
       workforce inclusion, representation, belonging, equitable practices, and DEI
       initiatives.

   •   Maintaining this distinction will help prevent duplication across the seven modules.

Instructional Guardrails

   •   Do not reveal how ER decisions mathematically affect Employee Satisfaction.

   •   Do not reveal the Engagement calculation.

   •   Do not reveal Turnover or Absenteeism formulas.

   •   Do not disclose HR Balance Scorecard weights.

   •   Do not identify ideal ER spending levels.

   •   Do not identify the “best” conflict-resolution strategy.

   •   Do not provide answers to workplace scenarios before students encounter them.

   •   Do not tell students which choices will maximize their simulation score.

The page should teach students what an HR leader needs to consider, while leaving the
actual judgment and consequences to the simulation.

Maintain the Standard Seven-Module Architecture

Continue using the same page architecture:

Module Introduction → What Is It? → Why Does It Matter? → Decisions You May
Encounter → Think Like an HR Leader → Connection to Organizational Outcomes →
Before You Enter the Simulation

This is becoming an important part of the design. Students should eventually recognize the
structure immediately regardless of which HR domain they are exploring.

                                       Page 33 of 420
Iteration #4 Boundary

   •   Keep the Employee Relations page lightweight and informational.

   •   Do not add legal case libraries, policy databases, investigation simulations, AI
       conflict coaching, quizzes, videos, or detailed scenario exercises during Iteration #4.

   •   Those could become powerful future instructional enhancements, particularly for
       Employee Relations, but they are outside the current development boundary.

   •   The immediate objective is to establish the orientation, learning scaffolding,
       navigation, and conceptual connections to the simulation.

Important Design Principle for Amit: Employee Relations is an especially good example
of why these pages should prepare rather than prescribe. Students need enough domain
knowledge to recognize issues of fairness, consistency, communication, trust, and
organizational consequences, but the simulation should still require them to exercise
professional judgment when the actual decision appears.

Overall learning objective: Before entering the simulation, students should understand
that Employee Relations is about how organizations manage the ongoing relationship
between employees and the workplace, and that the quality of those decisions can
influence trust, engagement, retention, productivity, risk, and broader organizational
performance over time.

This page provides a particularly important opportunity to begin building students' HR
financial acumen.




                                     Page 40 of 420
   •   Compensation & Benefits should be presented as both a workforce investment
       and a significant organizational cost.

       through:

Student C&B Decision → Simulation Engine → Workforce Outcomes → Financial
Outcomes → The Workforce Brief / P&L / Cash Flow / Financial Ratios

   •   Amit should simply structure the system so the same underlying values can later
       populate the appropriate financial reports.

       module, and the financial reports.

2. Why Does It Matter?

Aligns Structure with Strategy
A well-designed organization helps ensure that the appropriate people, roles,
responsibilities, and accountabilities are positioned to execute organizational strategy.

Improves Efficiency and Collaboration
Clear structures and effective processes can reduce unnecessary friction, improve
coordination, and help teams work together effectively.

Builds Agility and Adaptability
Effective organization design and change management can help the organization respond
to market shifts, technology, workforce changes, and other external forces.

Drives Organizational Performance

innovation, speed, quality, employee experience, and financial results.

   •   These should remain decision categories rather than recommended solutions.

   •   Ask students to consider tradeoffs involving:
           o Control versus flexibility
           o Efficiency versus adaptability
           o Short-term disruption versus long-term benefits
           o Cost versus organizational capability
           o Standardization versus responsiveness
           o Speed of change versus employee readiness
   •   Reinforce that the objective is not simply to restructure the organization, but to
       determine whether design and change choices support the organization's strategy
       and workforce.

Important Strategy Connection

This module should make the relationship between strategy and organization design
especially visible.

   •   Students should begin recognizing that organizational structure should support the
       strategy the organization is attempting to execute.
   •   However, the page should not tell students which organizational design is
       appropriate for a particular simulation strategy.
   •   The learning page should introduce the question:

Does the way we have organized people and work support what our organization is
trying to accomplish?

   •   The actual consequences of that alignment or misalignment should emerge
       through the simulation.

Change Should Have a Time Dimension

This is one area where I would give Amit an additional programming/design note
because it connects directly to the simulation engine.

                                    Page 49 of 420
   •   Organization-design and change decisions should conceptually support carry-
       forward effects across rounds.
   •   A restructuring or change initiative may create:
           o Immediate costs
           o Short-term disruption
           o Employee uncertainty
           o Future efficiency
           o Future capability
           o Improved adaptability
   •   The student-facing pre-simulation page should not expose these calculations.
   •   However, Amit should keep this temporal relationship in mind when connecting
       future OD&C decisions to the engine.

This reinforces one of the broader simulation principles:

Not every good HR decision produces an immediate positive result.

Cross-Module Relationships

Org Design & Change should be presented as highly interconnected with the other six
HR areas.

Conceptual relationships may include:

   •   Recruitment & Selection — structural changes may create new staffing and
       capability requirements.
   •   Performance Management — organizational roles and responsibilities influence
       performance expectations and accountability.
   •   Training & Development — organizational change may create new skill and
       capability requirements.
   •   Employee Relations — restructuring and change may affect employee trust,
       communication, concerns, and workplace climate.
   •   Compensation & Benefits — redesigned jobs and responsibilities may affect
       compensation requirements.
   •   DEI Initiatives — organization design may influence access, representation,
       participation, and inclusion.

Keep these relationships conceptual. The page should not reveal specific engine weights.

Before You Enter the Simulation

Maintain the standardized “Before You Enter the Simulation” section.

Use the three approved reflection questions:

   1. What design changes might help us execute our strategy more effectively?

                                      Page 50 of 420
   2. How will we manage change while keeping employees engaged and
      productive?
   3. How could our design and change decisions impact results in future rounds?

   •   These questions are for reflection and learning readiness.
   •   No student response submission is required during Iteration #4.
   •   They may later connect to Team Reflection, classroom discussion, or professor-
       facilitated debriefing.

Bottom Navigation

Maintain the standardized buttons:

   •   Explore Other HR Decision Modules
   •   Sign In to Begin
   •   Explore Other HR Decision Modules returns students to the Decision Module
       area.
   •   Sign In to Begin routes students to the simulation login.
   •   Maintain Amit's orange primary-action treatment for Sign In to Begin.

Instructional Guardrails

   •   Do not reveal organization-design scoring formulas.
   •   Do not identify an optimal organizational structure.
   •   Do not provide an ideal span of control.
   •   Do not identify the best restructuring decision.
   •   Do not reveal change-management multipliers.
   •   Do not show how specific design decisions mathematically affect Productivity,
       Engagement, or Financial Performance.
   •   Do not reveal carry-forward coefficients.
   •   Do not tell students which structure best matches each strategy.
   •   Do not provide the decisions necessary to maximize the HR Balance Scorecard.

The page should help students understand the managerial questions, not solve them.

Reusable Programming Architecture

At this point, I would strongly encourage Amit not to code this as another completely
independent page.

The six pages now share essentially the same underlying structure:

Module Introduction → What Is It? → Why Does It Matter? → Decisions You May
Encounter → Think Like an HR Leader → Connection to Organizational Outcomes
→ Before You Enter the Simulation


                                     Page 51 of 420
Ideally, Amit should create one reusable Pre-Simulation Learning Page
component/template and populate it with module-specific content.

The underlying content structure could conceptually contain:

Module Name | Subtitle | Icon | Hero Image | Introduction | What Is It Content |
Four Why-It-Matters Cards | Decision List | HR Leader Message | Decision
Outcomes | Workforce Outcomes | Organizational Outcomes | Closing Challenge |
Three Reflection Questions

That will make the seventh page—and future revisions—much easier to manage.

Iteration #4 Boundary

   •   Keep this page lightweight and informational.
   •   Do not add organizational-chart builders, restructuring tools, change-readiness
       assessments, scenario exercises, quizzes, videos, or AI change-management
       coaching yet.
   •   Those could become valuable later.
   •   The Iteration #4 priority remains establishing learning scaffolding, consistent
       navigation, reusable architecture, and conceptual preparation for simulation
       decision-making.

Important Design Principle for Amit: Org Design & Change should introduce students
to a different type of HR decision: one in which the consequences may not appear
immediately. Organizational redesign can create short-term disruption while potentially
generating longer-term capability. This page should prepare students to think about
strategy, systems, people, tradeoffs, and time without revealing how the engine
evaluates those decisions.

Overall learning objective: Before entering the simulation, students should understand
that organization design determines how work is structured, while change management
influences how people move from the current organization toward the desired
organization. Effective HR leadership requires considering both if the organization is
going to execute today while remaining capable of adapting tomorrow.

1. What Is DEI?

   •   Create the section “1. What Is DEI?”

   •   Explain DEI at an introductory level:

           o   Diversity considers differences in backgrounds, experiences, perspectives,
               identities, and representation.

           o   Equity considers fairness in access, opportunity, resources, and
               organizational practices.

           o   Inclusion considers whether people are able to participate, contribute, and
               experience belonging within the organization.

   •   Explain that DEI initiatives can involve:

           o   Representation

           o   Equitable practices

           o   Inclusion and belonging

           o   Accessibility

           o   Employee development

           o   Removal of organizational barriers

   •   Explain that students may eventually make decisions about how organizational
       resources and practices support these areas.

   •   Keep the section conceptual and introductory.



                                       Page 55 of 420
   •   Do not disclose DEI scoring formulas, thresholds, weights, or preferred investment
       levels.

2. Why Does It Matter?

Maintain the standardized four-card “Why Does It Matter?” architecture.

Strengthens Inclusion and Belonging
Inclusive environments can help employees feel valued, respected, supported, and able to
contribute effectively.

Expands Talent Access and Retention
Inclusive talent practices may broaden access to qualified candidates and support retention
across the workforce.

Improves Collaboration and Innovation
Different perspectives combined with an inclusive environment can contribute to stronger
problem solving, collaboration, creativity, and innovation.

Drives Organizational Performance
Effective DEI practices may influence engagement, productivity, quality, employee
experience, customer impact, and broader organizational results.

   •   Maintain the orange, blue, green, and purple visual treatment used in the approved
       page.

   •   Keep each explanation concise and easy to scan.

3. Decisions You May Encounter

Create the section “3. Decisions You May Encounter.”

Continue using “may encounter” so students receive learning scaffolding without being
shown the exact decisions that will appear in a particular round.

Potential decision areas include:

   •   Expanding diverse recruitment and talent pipelines

   •   Providing equity and unconscious-bias education

   •   Developing employee resource groups and inclusion networks

   •   Improving fair and equitable organizational policies and processes

   •   Addressing pay-equity and opportunity gaps

   •   Enhancing inclusive-leadership and manager capability

   •   Supporting accessibility and accommodation initiatives

                                      Page 56 of 420
   •   Balancing DEI investment against other HR priorities

These should be presented as categories of organizational decisions rather than
recommended actions.

Do not provide specific spending levels, thresholds, ideal DEI configurations, or decisions
that maximize simulation performance.

Think Like an HR Leader

   •   Retain the standardized orange-accented “Think Like an HR Leader” callout.

   •   Reinforce the approved message that DEI is more than a collection of programs;
       it involves ongoing organizational practices related to fairness, respect, opportunity,
       inclusion, and culture.

   •   Ask students to consider:

           o   Who has access to opportunities?

           o   How are policies experienced across the workforce?

           o   Are organizational practices fair and consistent?

           o   Do employees feel able to participate and contribute?

           o   How might current decisions affect future organizational capability?

   •   Reinforce that DEI decisions should be considered alongside strategy, workforce
       needs, organizational culture, and available resources.

4. Connecting DEI Initiatives to the Organization

Maintain the standardized three-stage visual architecture:

DEI Decisions → Workforce Outcomes → Organizational Outcomes

Under DEI Decisions, display:

   •   Diverse Recruitment

   •   Equity Practices

   •   Inclusion Initiatives

   •   Accessibility & Support

   •   Productivity

   •   Quality

   •   Maintain the standardized orange → blue → green visual progression.

   •   Do not reveal how specific DEI decisions mathematically affect the DEI Score,
       Engagement, Retention, Productivity, or HR Balance Scorecard.

   •   End the section with:

“The challenge is to build an inclusive culture where everyone can thrive and the
organization can achieve its goals.”

Important Cross-Module Integration

This page should particularly reinforce that DEI is interconnected with the other six HR
Decision Modules.

Conceptual relationships may include:

   •   Recruitment & Selection — access to talent pipelines, candidate pools, and
       selection practices.

   •   Performance Management — fairness and consistency in evaluation, feedback,
       development, and advancement.




                                        Page 58 of 420
   •   Training & Development — access to learning opportunities and inclusive-
       leadership capability.

   •   Employee Relations — perceptions of fairness, belonging, trust, workplace climate,
       and employee concerns.

   •   Compensation & Benefits — pay equity, benefit accessibility, and perceptions of
       total-rewards fairness.

   •   Org Design & Change — access to roles, decision-making, advancement
       opportunities, and participation during organizational change.

The student-facing page should explain these relationships conceptually. Do not expose
engine weights or causal formulas.

Important Distinction from Employee Relations

   •   Avoid making DEI Initiatives simply another Employee Relations page.

   •   Employee Relations primarily addresses the ongoing relationship between
       employees and the organization through workplace concerns, communication,
       conflict, policy, fairness, and workplace climate.

   •   DEI Initiatives should focus more specifically on representation, access, equity,
       inclusion, belonging, accessibility, and systemic organizational practices.

   •   Some overlap is expected because HR systems are interconnected, but the
       instructional purpose of the two modules should remain distinguishable.

      resources?

       other instructional activities.

Instructional Guardrails

   •   Do not display the DEI Score formula.

   •   Do not reveal DEI scoring weights.

   •   Do not identify an ideal DEI investment level.

   •   Do not identify the combination of initiatives producing the highest DEI Score.

   •   Do not reveal how DEI mathematically affects Engagement, Retention, Productivity,
       or other metrics.

   •   Do not provide answers to future simulation scenarios.

   •   Do not tell students which choices maximize the HR Balance Scorecard.

The page should help students understand what questions HR leaders need to consider
without telling them what decisions to make.

That is the larger Iteration #4 design objective: prepare students to think before asking
them to decide.

Student Landing Page – “Getting

Iteration #4 Design & Programming Notes for Amit

Dr. Cooper's Overall Observation

    •   The current student landing page is clean, easy to understand, and relatively
        easy to navigate. I do not want to overcomplicate what is already working.
    •   However, after reviewing the page more closely, I believe the purpose of the
        page needs to be more clearly defined.
    •   For now, the student landing page will officially be called Getting Started.
    •   The purpose of Getting Started is to answer one basic question for a student
        immediately after login:

“What do I need to know and do before my team begins making HR decisions?”

    •   This is important because Getting Started is not the Student Dashboard,
        Reports page, or HR Decision interface.
    •   It is the bridge between signing into the simulation and being prepared to
        participate in the simulation.
    •   I like the simplicity of the existing page. Keep that simplicity.
    •   The approved design image should be used as the visual reference for this
        iteration.

Getting Started = Page

Left Navigation = Application Navigation

Real HR Simulation Logo = Home

Simulator = Quick access to active simulation

This distinction should remain consistent as Amit builds additional pages.



21. Responsive Navigation
For desktop:

   •   Sidebar can remain expanded by default.
   •   Hamburger collapses it.

For tablet:

   •   Sidebar may collapse to icons or a drawer.

For mobile:

   •   Sidebar should remain hidden until the hamburger is selected.

This does not need sophisticated mobile optimization during Iteration #4, but Amit
should avoid programming the architecture in a way that prevents responsive
behavior later.



22. Dynamic vs. Static Content
Amit should distinguish between information that must eventually be dynamic and
instructional content that can initially remain static.

Dynamic

   •   Student name
   •   Student initials
   •   Role
   •   Course
   •   Semester

                                     Page 78 of 420
   •   Company
   •   Team
   •   Industry
   •   Strategy
   •   Current round
   •   Round status
   •   Orientation completion
   •   Team status
   •   HR learning-page status
   •   Simulation availability

Initially Static

   •   Introductory instructions
   •   What to Expect descriptions
   •   Help text
   •   Getting Started descriptions
   •   Important instructor-control message

This distinction should help avoid unnecessary backend complexity during Iteration #4.



23. Do Not Overbuild Progress Tracking Yet
The status system is valuable:

Complete | In Progress | Not Started | Waiting

However, I do not want the development of a sophisticated learning-management
tracking system to slow down this iteration.

For Iteration #4:

   •   Establish the status UI.
   •   Connect obvious statuses where easy.
   •   Round availability should connect to instructor controls.
   •   Other progress statuses can initially be simplified.

We can improve persistence and completion logic later.

That is the functional purpose of this page.




                                      Page 80 of 420
25. Connection to the Larger Learning Architecture
This page also clarifies the different levels of scaffolding we are beginning to build into
Real HR Simulation.

Layer 1 – Public Pre-Simulation Learning

What does HR do?

Seven HR Decision Module learning pages.

Layer 2 – Getting Started

How does this simulation work, and what organization am I managing?

Orientation + Company + Industry + Strategy + HR environment.

Layer 3 – Simulation Decision Environment

What decisions should our team make?

Students exercise judgment and make HR decisions.

Layer 4 – Reports & HR Analytics

What happened?

The Workforce Brief, HR Balance Scorecard, KPIs, workforce metrics, and financial
reports.

Layer 5 – Feedback & Reflection

What might we learn from what happened?

Perspective Feedback + Workforce Feedback + HR Coaching + Team Reflection.

Layer 6 – Next Round

What will we do differently?

Students apply what they learned to subsequent decisions.

This larger architecture does not all need to be programmed during Iteration #4, but
Amit should understand where Getting Started fits within the broader design.

26. Iteration #4 Programming Priority
For this page, prioritize:

    1. Getting Started page layout.
    2. Collapsible left navigation.
    3. Dynamic student identification.
    4. Dynamic Your Simulation information.
    5. Five-step Getting Started structure.
    6. Active Welcome & Orientation link.
    7. Existing Join Session/team connection.
    8. Industry & Strategy Brief link.
    9. Connection to the seven HR learning pages.
    10. Instructor-controlled Enter Simulation status.
    11. Reports renamed Reports & HR Analytics.
    12. Basic Help functionality.
    13. Consistent navigation across authenticated student pages.

Do not allow this page to create unnecessary scope creep.

Update to the navigation not included above.

    •   Update the authenticated student navigation to the following order: Getting
        Started → Dashboard → HR Decisions → Review & Submit → Reports &
        HR Analytics → Team & Company → Resources → Help Center.
    •   Dashboard is now a separate function from Getting Started. Getting Started
        prepares the student; Dashboard becomes the operational home once the
        simulation begins.
    •   HR Decisions, Reports & HR Analytics, Team & Company, Resources, and
        Help Center should support expandable submenus as functionality develops.
    •   Review & Submit should remain a direct primary navigation item because it
        represents a distinct and important step before decisions are committed.
    •   Highlight the student's current page in orange so location within the simulation
        is always clear.
    •   Retain the collapsible sidebar/hamburger functionality across all authenticated
        student pages.
    •   Once the student enters an active round, clicking the Real HR Simulation logo
        should route to Dashboard; Getting Started remains accessible from the menu.
    •   The left navigation should become the standard navigation architecture across
        the entire student simulation environment, eliminating the need for redundant
        Quick Links on individual pages.

Dr. Cooper's Overall Design Comment to Amit
Amit, I really like where this page is going. The current design is clean and does not need
to become more complicated simply because we can add more functionality. The major
change for me is that we now have a clearer purpose for Getting Started. This should be
the student's preparation space after login—not another dashboard. The student should
immediately know who they are, what company they are managing, what industry and
strategy they have, what they need to review, and whether they are ready to enter the
simulation.

throughout the application. However, making it collapsible gives us the best of both
designs: the clean interface I like and the navigation structure we need as the simulation
becomes more complex.

Most importantly, do not overbuild this iteration. We are establishing the architecture.
We can add sophistication later after we validate the engine, student workflow, and
testing environment.

Core Design Principle

Getting Started should prepare students to participate—not attempt to run the
simulation from the landing page.

That distinction gives this page a very clear purpose and gives Amit a cleaner
programming boundary for Iteration #4.

Recruitment & Selection – HR Decisions
Page
Iteration #4 Design & Programming Notes for Amit

   •   Use the approved Recruitment & Selection image as the visual design
       reference. The objective is to make the decision environment significantly more
       graphical, intuitive, and easier to scan while preserving the current engine inputs
       and calculations.
   •   Do not simplify or remove the structural logic inputs. The redesign concerns
       presentation and workflow—not the underlying Recruitment engine.
   •   The HR Decisions pages should be intentionally less busy than Getting Started,
       Dashboard, or Reports & HR Analytics. Once students reach this area, their
       cognitive effort should be focused primarily on making decisions.


                                     Page 95 of 420
  •   Maintain the standard authenticated navigation on the left: Getting Started;
      Dashboard; HR Decisions; Review & Submit; Reports & HR Analytics;
      Team & Company; Resources; Help Center.
  •   Make HR Decisions expandable and display all seven official module names
      beneath it: Recruitment & Selection; Performance Management; Training &
      Development; Employee Relations; Compensation & Benefits; Org Design &
      Change; DEI Initiatives.
  •   Use these titles consistently throughout the entire simulation. Eliminate shortened
      labels such as Recruitment, Performance, Training, Relations, and Compensation.
  •   Highlight HR Decisions and Recruitment & Selection in orange when the
      student is working on this page.
  •   Retain the student profile, Quick Links, and compact Your Simulation panel in
      the left navigation so students maintain persistent context while making decisions.

Page Header and Round Context

  •   Display Recruitment & Selection as the primary page heading.
  •   Retain the small professional alignment indicator as SHRM BASK · Talent
      Acquisition.
  •   Keep the existing short explanation of the module, but do not add extensive
      instructional material. The Pre-Simulation Learning page already serves that
      purpose.
  •   Display a compact round-information strip showing Round, Industry, Strategy,
      Economy, and HR Decisions progress.
  •   Example: Round 1 – Open | Manufacturing | Focus | Normal | HR Decisions 1
      of 7.
  •   All information should populate dynamically from the current simulation/session
      configuration.

Discretionary HR Budget

  •   Keep the Discretionary HR Budget visible near the top of every HR Decision
      page.
  •   Display:
          o Total budget
          o Amount currently used
          o Amount remaining
          o Percentage used
  •   Represent this graphically using a horizontal progress bar rather than relying
      primarily on text.
  •   The budget bar should update immediately as decisions are changed across any of
      the seven HR modules.
  •   The budget shown here must be the same centralized budget value used by the
      engine and Dashboard, not a separately calculated Recruitment budget.

Decision Guidance

                                    Page 96 of 420
  •   Retain the existing warning/range functionality because it is important for student
      decision-making.
  •   Replace the current long series of global warning messages with a compact
      Decision Guidance panel.
  •   On the Recruitment & Selection page, show only:
          o Recruitment-specific warnings
          o Overall budget warnings that materially affect the full HR decision
              environment
  •   Performance warnings should appear under Performance Management; Training
      warnings under Training & Development; Compensation warnings under
      Compensation & Benefits, etc.
  •   This should significantly reduce visual clutter while preserving the existing
      warning logic.
  •   Use neutral guidance such as:
          o Below Suggested Range
          o Within Suggested Range
          o Above Suggested Range
  •   Do not tell students what action to take.
  •   Use language such as: “Consider whether your current investment supports
      your workforce and strategic needs.”
  •   Avoid language such as: “Increase Recruitment spending.”

Industry Guidance

  •   Retain the current Manufacturing Recruitment suggested range of 15%–22% of
      HR budget as currently configured.
  •   Represent the range graphically.
  •   Show:
          o Minimum suggested percentage
          o Maximum suggested percentage
          o Student/team's current investment marker
  •   Clearly label the range as Suggested Industry Range, never optimal or required.
  •   Industry guidance should dynamically change when the professor selects a
      different industry.
  •   The range display should be visually understandable without requiring students to
      interpret a paragraph.

Module Investment

  •   Add a compact Module Investment card for Recruitment & Selection.
  •   Display:
         o Current Recruitment investment in dollars
         o Percentage of total HR budget
         o Suggested industry range
         o Graphical position relative to that range
  •   This should update dynamically as Recruitment decisions change.

                                    Page 97 of 420
   •   Avoid duplicating calculations; use the same engine-derived values that support
       budget warnings and reporting.

Decision Structure

   •   Organize Recruitment & Selection into four clear decision cards:
           1. Hiring Needs
           2. Selection Approach
           3. Diversity Sourcing Goal
           4. Onboarding Investment
   •   Keep all four areas visible within one primary decision workspace whenever
       screen size permits.
   •   The objective is for students to see the complete Recruitment decision structure at
       a glance.

1. Hiring Needs
   •   Retain all five existing role groups because they are required for the structural
       logic engine:
           o Entry-Level / Support
           o Professional / Individual Contributor
           o Technical / Specialist
           o Manager / Supervisor
           o Senior / Executive
   •   Retain the short examples underneath each category, but make them secondary
       visually.
   •   Replace plain numeric input boxes with intuitive minus / number / plus controls
       where technically practical.
   •   Example: − 2 +
   •   Prevent negative hiring values.
   •   Retain reasonable input validation.
   •   Add a dynamically calculated TOTAL NEW HIRES at the bottom of the card.
   •   In the current example, the five role inputs total 5 new hires.

2. Selection Approach
   •   Retain Screening Rigor as a structural-engine input.
   •   Continue using the existing configured options.
   •   Present it as a clear dropdown.
   •   Example: Assessment + Interview ▼
   •   Include only a short neutral descriptor, such as: “More rigorous selection may
       require additional time and resources.”
   •   Do not identify which screening choice produces the best Hiring Quality or score.



                                     Page 98 of 420
3. Diversity Sourcing Goal
   •   Retain the existing percentage input and slider logic.
   •   Rename the student-facing label to Diversity Sourcing Goal for clarity.
   •   Display the selected percentage prominently above the slider.
   •   Example: 15%.
   •   Retain the slider as the primary graphical input.
   •   Continue feeding the exact selected percentage into the existing structural logic
       engine.
   •   Do not expose the mathematical relationship between this choice and the DEI
       Score, Hiring Quality, or other engine outcomes.

4. Onboarding Investment
   •   Retain Onboarding Investment per Hire as an engine input.
   •   Display the current dollar amount clearly.
   •   Example: $500 per Hire.
   •   Use plus/minus controls or the current numeric input depending on what is
       technically most reliable.
   •   Add a simple calculation underneath:
       5 New Hires × $500 = $2,500 Estimated Onboarding Investment
   •   This calculation helps students understand the financial consequence of their
       decision without revealing scoring formulas.

Decision Impact Preview

   •   Rename Live Preview to Decision Impact Preview.
   •   Retain the three existing Recruitment outputs currently calculated by the
       simulation:
          o Total Recruitment Cost
          o Cost per Hire
          o Estimated Time to Fill
   •   Display these as three prominent graphical KPI cards rather than a single text line.
   •   Example:
          o Total Recruitment Cost – $39,213
          o Cost per Hire – $7,843
          o Estimated Time to Fill – 34 Days
   •   These should update immediately as students change the Recruitment inputs.
   •   Continue to treat these values as estimates/previews, not final round results.
   •   Do not add Hiring Quality to the preview until its underlying engine logic has
       been fully validated.

Important Distinction: Preview vs. Results


                                     Page 99 of 420
  •   The Decision Impact Preview should help students understand the immediate
      projected implications of the inputs they are entering.
  •   It should not reveal the full final consequences of the round.
  •   Final outcomes still depend on the simulation engine, interconnected HR
      decisions, industry, strategy, economic conditions, carry-forward effects, and
      other variables.
  •   Therefore, do not expose final Scorecard results or full Workforce Performance
      Metrics on the decision page.

Auto-Save and Save Status

  •   Retain the current auto-save functionality.
  •   Reduce the visual prominence of Changes auto-save.
  •   Use a small status indicator such as:
           o ✓ All changes auto-saved
           o Saving…
  •   If the auto-save function is reliable, the Save Now button can eventually be
      removed.
  •   For Iteration #4, retain Save Now if Amit needs it for testing and recovery
      reliability.

Bottom Navigation
  •   Remove Quick Submit from the Recruitment page.
  •   Students should not be able to bypass the intentional Review & Submit stage.
  •   Use three clear actions:
         o ← Dashboard
         o Save Now (temporary if needed)
         o Save & Continue → Performance Management
  •   Make Save & Continue the primary orange action.
  •   The next module should automatically follow the approved order.

Seven-Module Decision Progress

  •   Display HR Decisions 1 of 7 at the top.
  •   As students progress:
         o Recruitment & Selection = 1 of 7
         o Performance Management = 2 of 7
         o Training & Development = 3 of 7
         o Employee Relations = 4 of 7
         o Compensation & Benefits = 5 of 7
         o Org Design & Change = 6 of 7
         o DEI Initiatives = 7 of 7
  •   Consider a subtle graphical progress indicator, but keep it lightweight.
  •   This is primarily a navigation/progress cue, not another scoring mechanism.

                                   Page 100 of 420
Reusable HR Decision Page Architecture

   •   Do not independently design seven completely different interfaces.
   •   Establish a common HR Decision Page shell containing:
           o Global left navigation
           o HR Decisions submenu
           o Module title
           o SHRM BASK label
           o Round/industry/strategy/economy context
           o Budget bar
           o Decision Guidance
           o Industry Guidance
           o Module Investment
           o Module-specific decision inputs
           o Decision Impact Preview
           o Save/Continue controls
   •   Each module can then populate its own inputs and preview metrics within this
       common framework.
   •   This will make the student experience significantly more consistent and reduce
       future programming/maintenance work.

Engine Integrity

   •   Do not create new UI calculations when an equivalent calculation already
       exists in the engine.
   •   Recruitment Cost, Cost per Hire, Time to Fill, budget used, remaining budget,
       investment percentage, and range warnings should all draw from centralized
       engine/session values.
   •   Changing the visual interface should not change the existing structural logic
       unless a separate engine revision is specifically approved.
   •   Professor/Admin diagnostic functionality should eventually allow us to trace each
       preview value back to the variables that produced it.

Iteration #4 Boundary
   •   Do not add advanced decision recommendations.
   •   Do not add HR Coach functionality to the decision page.
   •   Do not add detailed metric explanations.
   •   Do not expose engine formulas.
   •   Do not add final Scorecard estimates.
   •   Do not add extensive instructional material.
   •   Do not add unnecessary animations or complex visualizations.
   •   The priority is clarity, navigation, graphical decision inputs, preserved
       warning/range logic, and reliable connection to the existing engine.



                                    Page 101 of 420
Dr. Cooper's Overall Comment to Amit
Amit, I really like this direction for the HR Decision pages. The current Recruitment page
already contains much of the information and structural logic we need, so I do not want
us changing the engine simply to achieve a new visual design. The objective is to make
the student's interaction with that logic much more intuitive.

Once the student enters HR Decisions, the interface should get out of the way. Students
should immediately see what decisions they need to make, where their current investment
sits relative to suggested ranges, whether there are important warnings they should
consider, what their available budget is, and what the immediate estimated consequences
of their inputs may be.

I especially want to preserve the industry ranges and warnings. Those are important
instructional elements. What changes is how we present them: graphically, locally to the
relevant HR module, and without overwhelming students with warnings from unrelated
decision areas.

The structural-engine inputs remain the priority. The design simply makes those inputs
easier to understand and use.

The guiding design principle for all seven HR Decision pages should be:

Give students enough information to make an informed decision, but not enough
information to remove the uncertainty, judgment, and tradeoffs that make the
simulation a learning experience.

The role-level configuration is an important part of the engine and learning design.
