Reusable Architecture
Employee Relations now confirms that the common HR Decisions architecture works
across very different kinds of HR decisions.

The first four modules involve very different inputs:

Recruitment → people counts and sourcing/selection investments
Performance → systems and criteria weights
Training → program participation and development investments
Employee Relations → policies, programs, workplace practices, and investments

Yet all four can use the same shell:

Investment → Decisions → Impact Preview → Save & Continue

That is exactly the consistency I would want Amit to preserve for the remaining three
modules.

Dr. Cooper's Overall Comment to Amit
Amit, I really like this page. Employee Relations demonstrates that the common HR
Decision architecture can work even when the decisions are very different from
Recruitment, Performance, or Training. We are preserving the four existing structural
inputs—Engagement Investment, Conflict Approach, Flexibility, and Voice
Mechanisms—but presenting them in a way that makes the professional judgment behind
each decision much clearer to students.

I particularly like the four-card structure because students can immediately understand
what they are being asked to decide. The contextual information underneath each
decision should help students think about potential tradeoffs without telling them what
answer to select.

As before, preserve the engine. The design image should not create new formulas or
ranges. Where the image contains example investment ranges or Decision Impact
Preview values, only populate those values when the underlying engine logic has been
validated.

The more we build these pages, the more important consistency becomes. Students
should never have to relearn how to use the simulation when they move from one HR
function to another. They should only have to think differently about the HR decision
itself.

Core design principle: Keep the interface consistent so the complexity comes from the
HR judgment—not from learning how to navigate each page.

15. Financial Impact Should Be Allowed to Be Negative
This is especially important for Compensation.

The simulation should not imply:

More Compensation = Better Result

A stronger Total Rewards package could potentially:

    •   improve retention,
    •   reduce turnover,
    •   improve motivation,

while simultaneously:

    •   increasing payroll,
    •   increasing benefit expense,
    •   increasing bonus expense,
    •   reducing short-term profit.

That tradeoff is exactly what makes this an authentic strategic HR decision.

The Decision Impact Preview should therefore allow students to see something like:

Retention ↑

while simultaneously seeing:

Financial Impact ↓

That is good simulation design.

Dr. Cooper's Overall Comment to Amit
Amit, I really like this page. Compensation & Benefits is more financially complex than
the previous HR Decision areas, but the redesign makes those decisions much easier to
understand without oversimplifying them. I especially like the Compensation Economics
section because students can now see that their Total Rewards decisions have an
organizational financial consequence.

Please preserve the five separate salary decisions by role, along with Benefits, Bonus
Tier, Equity/Stock Options, and HR Technology. These are important structural inputs
and should not be consolidated simply to make the interface easier to program.

One important distinction on this page is the difference between the Discretionary HR
Budget and total organizational compensation costs. Base payroll, benefits, bonuses, and
equity may produce millions of dollars in estimated annual compensation costs, but those
amounts should not automatically be treated as spending against the $500,000
discretionary HR budget unless that is specifically how the engine is designed.

I also want the Decision Impact Preview to reinforce strategic tradeoffs. A student might
improve retention and reduce turnover through a stronger compensation package while
simultaneously increasing organizational costs. That is not a problem with the
simulation—that is exactly the type of strategic HR judgment we want students to
experience.



                                      Page 154 of 420
Finally, continue the same principle we have used throughout this iteration: the image
gives us the interface architecture, but the engine determines the numbers. Do not hard-
code example impact values unless those calculations have been validated.

Core design principle: Compensation should not teach students to spend more—it
should teach them to balance workforce competitiveness, employee outcomes, and
organizational financial performance.

Discretionary HR Budget

$223,550 / $500,000

$276,450 Remaining | 45% Used

with the graphical progress bar.

   •   Org Design & Change investments should update the centralized HR budget

   •   Do not create a separate independent budget.
   •   Not every organizational design decision necessarily needs a direct dollar cost.
   •   Structure Type, Span of Control, and Process Focus should primarily

                                     Page 157 of 420
Critical Engine Design Principle – These Decisions
Must Involve Tradeoffs
This is particularly important for this module.

   •   Do not program Organizational Structure, Process Focus, Change
       Management Capability, or Collaboration Enablement as simple Low →
       High → Better choices.
   •   There should not be one universally correct organizational design.
   •   The effectiveness of a choice should depend on its fit with the organization's
       strategy, industry, environment, workforce, and other HR decisions.
   •   Students should be learning the principle of organizational fit, not learning to
       select the most expensive or most sophisticated option.

For example:

Functional Structure

   •   May support specialization, consistency, and efficiency.
   •   May create silos or slower cross-functional coordination.

Matrix Structure

   •   May strengthen cross-functional coordination and flexibility.
   •   May introduce role ambiguity and complexity.

Neither is automatically "better."

The engine should eventually ask:

How well does this organizational design fit this organization's circumstances?

rather than:

Did the student select the highest-level option?

This principle should apply throughout this module.



1. Organizational Structure
Create the first decision card:

                                     Page 158 of 420
Organizational Structure

Prompt:

Choose the structure that best supports your strategy and operational needs.

Dropdown options:

   •   Functional
   •   Divisional
   •   Matrix
   •   Team-Based
   •   Flat

Default/example in the approved design:

Functional

Important Logic

Each structure should have strengths and tradeoffs.

Conceptually:

   •   Functional → specialization and efficiency
   •   Divisional → market/product responsiveness and accountability
   •   Matrix → cross-functional integration and flexibility
   •   Team-Based → collaboration and adaptability
   •   Flat → speed and empowerment

But each should also carry potential disadvantages.

Do not display all engine consequences to students.

Instead, provide a short neutral information cue based on the selected option.

Example for Functional:

Functional structures can support efficiency and specialization but may reduce
cross-functional coordination.

Changing the selection should dynamically change the informational cue.




                                     Page 159 of 420
2. Span of Control
Create:

Span of Control

Prompt:

Set the average number of direct reports per manager.

Use the graphical numeric control:

−6+

Do not use a dropdown.

   •      Establish reasonable minimum and maximum values.
   •      The approved image uses 6 as the example.
   •      The displayed Recommended: 4–8 should be treated as a design example until
          we formally validate the range.
   •      Do not hard-code 4–8 as an engine rule solely because it appears in the mockup.

Tradeoff Logic

Span of control is another decision that should not have a universally optimal value.

Narrower spans may support:

   •      Coaching
   •      Supervision
   •      Manager attention

but may increase:

   •      Management layers
   •      Administrative cost
   •      Bureaucracy

Wider spans may support:

   •      Efficiency
   •      Empowerment
   •      Flatter structures

but may reduce:

                                      Page 160 of 420
   •      Coaching capacity
   •      Oversight
   •      Manager availability

The engine should eventually evaluate the span in combination with structure,
workforce characteristics, strategy, and other organizational conditions.



3. Process Focus
Create:

Process Focus

Prompt:

Choose the primary focus for your process management approach.

Dropdown options:

   •      Efficiency
   •      Quality
   •      Innovation
   •      Customer Responsiveness
   •      Agility

Approved-image example:

Efficiency

Critical Logic

These choices should represent strategic priorities, not levels.

Do not score:

Efficiency = 1, Quality = 2, Innovation = 3...

These are categorical strategic choices.

Their effectiveness should depend on fit.

For example:



                                     Page 161 of 420
Efficiency may support:

   •      standardization
   •      cost control
   •      productivity

but may reduce flexibility if overemphasized.

Innovation may support:

   •      experimentation
   •      adaptability
   •      new ideas

but may create greater cost or process variability.

Agility may support:

   •      rapid response
   •      adaptability

but could reduce standardization.

Again:

Different does not mean better or worse.

   •      Basic
   •      Moderate
   •      Strong
   •      Advanced

                                      Page 162 of 420
Approved-image example:

Important Difference From the First Three Decisions

This decision can have an investment/cost progression.

Higher capability may require greater investment in:

   •      Change communication
   •      Manager preparation
   •      Employee involvement
   •      Training
   •      Change support
   •      Implementation resources

However:

Higher still should not automatically equal better.

Advanced capability may be unnecessary and expensive in a stable environment with
limited organizational change.

Strong capability may become much more valuable when:

   •      restructuring,
   •      implementing major technology,
   •      changing strategy,
   •      altering organizational structure,
   •      or operating in a highly dynamic environment.

This creates the tradeoff:

Capability Benefit vs. Investment Cost

Important Development Distinction for Amit
For the first five redesigned HR modules, our direction has generally been:

Preserve the engine; redesign the interface.

For Org Design & Change, the direction is different:

Design the interface AND establish new engine inputs—but do not finalize scoring
relationships until we have validated the conceptual logic.

Therefore, I would approach this in stages:

   1.  Build the page shell.
   2.  Add the five inputs.
   3.  Store the selections reliably.
   4.  Confirm they persist by team/round.
   5.  Establish cost logic for Change Management Capability and Collaboration
       Enablement.
   6. Establish configurable industry/strategy parameters.
   7. Develop the basic outcome relationships.
   8. Test the relationships structurally.
   9. Only then activate Decision Impact Preview values.
   10. Later test cross-module interactions.

This prevents the interface design from getting ahead of the engine.

                                     Page 170 of 420
Iteration #4 Boundary
Do not add:

   •   Organizational charts
   •   Department-by-department restructuring
   •   Individual reporting relationships
   •   Position elimination
   •   Layoff decisions
   •   Job redesign
   •   Individual role descriptions
   •   Detailed workflow mapping
   •   Change-project timelines
   •   Stakeholder mapping
   •   Change resistance simulations
   •   Employee-by-employee change reactions
   •   AI organizational-design recommendations
   •   New BSC scoring formulas before validation

For Version 1 of this section, the five proposed decisions provide enough strategic
complexity.



Dr. Cooper's Overall Comment to Amit
Amit, this page is somewhat different from the first five HR Decision pages because Org
Design & Change does not currently exist as an input area in the simulation. Therefore,
we are not simply redesigning existing inputs—we are beginning to establish a new part
of the structural engine.

I like the five proposed decisions because they capture important organizational-design
choices without making the module unnecessarily complicated: Organizational Structure,
Span of Control, Process Focus, Change Management Capability, and Collaboration
Enablement.

The most important programming principle for this area is that these cannot become
simple higher-is-better decisions. A Matrix structure is not automatically better than a
Functional structure. Innovation is not automatically better than Efficiency. A wider span
of control is not automatically better than a narrower one. Even Advanced Change
Management Capability may create unnecessary cost when the organization does not
require that level of capability.



                                     Page 171 of 420
What we eventually want the engine to evaluate is fit. Does the organizational design
make sense given the company's industry, strategy, economy, workforce, and other HR
decisions? That is a much more authentic organizational-design problem.

For this iteration, establish the page, inputs, persistence, and basic cost architecture first.
The investment ranges and Decision Impact Preview numbers shown in the image are
placeholders until we develop and validate the underlying structural logic. I would rather
have the interface show a dash than have us create an authoritative-looking number that
the engine cannot yet support.

Core design principle: There is no universally “best” organizational design. The
learning comes from selecting a configuration that fits the organization's strategy,
environment, workforce, and operating needs—and understanding the tradeoffs that come
with that choice.

   •      Employee support systems

Information Cue

                                      Page 179 of 420
Accessibility and support ensure all employees can contribute and thrive.

Important DEI Engine Principle – Portfolio Coherence
This is the most important conceptual point for this module.

Do not program these five choices as independent “higher = better” variables.

We want students to think about the coherence of their DEI strategy.

For example:

A company might select:

Advanced Diverse Recruitment

but:

Minimal Inclusion Initiatives

That organization may successfully attract diverse talent but struggle to create an
environment that retains that talent.

Similarly:

Advanced DEI Training

combined with:

Minimal Equity Practices

should not automatically produce excellent DEI outcomes.

The simulation should eventually distinguish between:

Representation → Equity → Inclusion → Belonging → Retention

This creates a much richer learning experience.

Avoid the “Spend More = Better DEI” Problem
Higher investment levels may provide stronger capability, but they should also create:

   •   Higher costs
   •   Implementation demands

                                     Page 180 of 420
   •   Managerial capacity requirements
   •   Potential diminishing returns

Therefore:

Advanced + Advanced + Advanced + Advanced + Highly Integrated

should not automatically be the optimal answer.

Students must balance DEI investments against:

   •   Other HR priorities
   •   Available budget
   •   Workforce conditions
   •   Strategy
   •   Industry
   •   Existing HR systems

This is consistent with the strategic tradeoff logic we established for Org Design &
Change.

Dr. Cooper’s Overall Comment to Amit
Amit, I really like this page and think it completes the seven HR Decision areas very
well. The five proposed DEI decisions—Diverse Recruitment & Talent Pipelines, Equity
Practices, Inclusion Initiatives, Training & Education, and Accessibility & Support—
connect directly with the learning architecture we established on the DEI Pre-Simulation
Learning page without making the decision environment unnecessarily complicated.

The most important engine principle here is that DEI cannot become a simple spend-
more-and-score-higher variable. I want students thinking about the coherence of their
DEI strategy. For example, an organization could invest heavily in attracting diverse
talent but make very little investment in inclusion or equitable practices. That
organization may improve representation while still struggling with employee experience
and retention. That is exactly the kind of strategic relationship the simulation should
eventually help students understand.



                                    Page 187 of 420
DEI also gives us an important opportunity for future cross-module integration because
these decisions naturally connect with Recruitment, Performance Management, Training,
Employee Relations, Compensation, and Org Design & Change. We do not need to build
all those interactions now. First establish the five inputs, their costs, persistence, and
basic structural logic.

examples for the interface. Do not hard-code the 4%–8% range, $11,650 investment, or
Decision Impact Preview values until we have validated those relationships in the engine.

Finally, because this is Decision 7 of 7, completing this page should transition the student
out of decision-making and into Review & Submit. That gives us a clear end-to-end
student workflow.

Core design principle: DEI effectiveness should emerge from the coherence and fit of
the organization's talent, equity, inclusion, development, and accessibility practices—not
simply from how much money the team spends.

                                       Page 203 of 420
22. Strategic Inconsistency Is the Learning Opportunity
This is one of the biggest reasons for redesigning this page.

Students should be able to visually notice situations such as:

High Recruitment Investment + Very Low Compensation

or:

High Training Investment + Low Succession Investment

or:

Strong Diverse Recruitment + Minimal Inclusion Initiatives

or:

Matrix Structure + Limited Collaboration Enablement

or:

Advanced Change Capability + No meaningful organizational change

The system does not necessarily need to explicitly tell students:

“Your strategy is inconsistent.”

The visual layout should help them see the pattern themselves.

That is much more educational.



23. Do Not Over-Coach the Student
The Review page should provide:

      •   Decisions
      •   Investment
      •   Industry guidance
      •   Warnings
      •   Projected consequences

It should not tell students what combination to choose.

                                     Page 204 of 420
Avoid:

Recommended Change: Reduce Training to 8%.

Instead:

Training investment exceeds the suggested Manufacturing range.

Then let the student decide whether the investment is strategically justified.

                                    Page 206 of 420
28. Team Submission Logic
Because students are operating as teams, we eventually need to confirm who has
authority to submit.

For Version 1, if any team member can submit:

    •   Clearly tell students that submission locks decisions for the entire team.

If only a designated team member can submit:

    •   Disable the Submit button for other members and explain why.

This needs to be intentional rather than accidental behavior.



29. Data Integrity Requirement
Review & Submit should not create another copy of the team's decisions.

It should read directly from the same saved team/round state used by each HR Decision
module.

Conceptually:

Saved HR Decisions

↓

Review & Submit

↓

Engine Preview

↓

Final Submit

This means if a student edits Training & Development and returns, the Review page
should immediately show the updated:

    •   Training decisions
    •   Training investment

                                     Page 207 of 420
   •      Total budget
   •      Cost breakdown
   •      Warnings
   •      Projected outcomes

No separate synchronization process should be necessary.



30. Review & Submit Is Not Reports & HR Analytics
Maintain this boundary:

Review & Submit

What are we about to do?

versus:

Reports & HR Analytics

What happened after we did it?

Review & Submit contains projected consequences.

The Workforce Brief contains actual round results.

That distinction should remain throughout the architecture.



31. Iteration #4 Boundary
For this page, do not add:

   •      AI recommendations
   •      Automatic strategy correction
   •      Optimization suggestions
   •      Detailed financial statements
   •      Full Workforce Brief metrics
   •      Historical round comparisons
   •      Competitor results
   •      Leaderboard
   •      HR Coaching
   •      Team Reflection

                                      Page 208 of 420
   •   Complex scenario analysis
   •   “Optimal decision” indicators

The page should remain a clear, graphical quality-control checkpoint.



Dr. Cooper's Overall Comment to Amit
Amit, this is a significant improvement to the current Review & Submit page. The
existing page contains much of the information we need—budget, spending, warnings,
Compensation Economics, and projected outcomes—but it currently reads more like a
report than a final decision checkpoint. The redesign should make the student's entire HR
strategy visible in one place.

The most important addition is the seven HR Decision summary cards. Before students
submit, they should be able to scan Recruitment, Performance, Training, Employee
Relations, Compensation, Org Design & Change, and DEI and ask themselves whether
those decisions make sense together. If something looks inconsistent, they should be able
to click Edit, return directly to that module, make the change, and come right back to
Review & Submit.

I also want us to preserve the warnings and projected consequences already available in
the current engine, but present them much more graphically. We should consolidate
duplicate warnings and allow students to connect a warning directly back to the HR
Decision that produced it.

Most importantly, Review & Submit should not make the decision for the student. It
should make the strategy visible enough that students can identify potential
inconsistencies themselves. That reflection is part of the learning experience.

Finally, Submit Decisions needs to be treated as a meaningful system event. Saving is not
submitting. Once students confirm submission, their team's decisions should lock for that
round, the Dashboard should update, and they should move into the waiting-for-results
stage of the simulation.

Core Design Principle

The seven HR Decision pages help students make individual HR choices. Review &
Submit should help them see whether those individual choices form a coherent HR
strategy before they commit the organization to the round.

Workforce Brief – Iteration Changes
 •   Rename the official simulation report from a generic “Reports” output to The
     Workforce Brief, establishing a consistent branded report students receive after
     each simulation round.
 •   Create a round-based report menu on the left side of the page, allowing

 •   Reduce the report-selection header footprint by positioning “The Workforce

     using a large separate section across the main page.
 •   Add strategic financial report navigation across the top with links to The
     Workforce Brief, Balance Sheet, Profit & Loss Statement, Cash Flow
     Statement, and Financial Ratio Reports. These can initially function as
     placeholders until the financial reporting functionality is developed.
 •   Rename “Total Round Score” to “HR Balance Scorecard.” Display the overall
     score prominently as a percentage (e.g., 52.3%) representing the overall strategic
     effectiveness of the team's HR decisions for the round.

Additional Design & Programming Notes for Amit
  •   Use the approved Workforce Brief image as the primary visual design reference.
  •   The Workforce Brief is a post-round results page. Students should only see finalized
      results after the round has been processed/released.
  •   Prior-round Workforce Briefs should remain read-only and permanently accessible.
      Students should not be able to alter historical results.
  •   All results displayed on the page should pull from the same finalized round-result
      dataset used by Reports & HR Analytics. Do not independently calculate values on this
      page.
  •   Maintain exact terminology between The Workforce Brief, HR Metrics Reference,
      Dashboard, and other Reports & HR Analytics pages. For example, if the
      authoritative metric is “Turnover Rate,” do not call it “Turnover” elsewhere.
  •   Only display validated engine metrics. Do not create additional metrics merely to
      complete a visual category.
  •   Perspective Summary and Workforce Performance feedback should be interpretive but
      developmental. Feedback can identify what students should examine, but should not
      reveal engine formulas, weights, multipliers, or optimal decisions.
  •   Clearly distinguish actual results in The Workforce Brief from the estimated outcomes
      students saw in Decision Impact Previews before submission.
  •   The financial-report tabs—Balance Sheet, Profit & Loss Statement, Cash Flow
      Statement, and Financial Ratios—should use the same finalized round data and remain
      accessible by round once those reports are programmed.
  •   HR Coach – Coming Soon should remain visual only during Iteration #4. Do not build
      AI functionality yet.
  •   Team Reflection should be saved by Team + Round, so each round retains its own
      reflection. The reflection should not change the team's score or simulation outcomes.
  •   Consider making Team Reflection editable until the next round begins and then read-only
      afterward; this can be finalized later.
  •   Where appropriate, metric names or information icons can eventually link directly to the
      corresponding Resources → HR Metrics Reference definition rather than duplicating
      explanations on this page.
  •   Likewise, an Understanding The Workforce Brief link can route students to the
      existing Resources → Simulation Reference Center article.
  •   Do not add Save Now for simulation results because results are read-only. Only Team
      Reflection requires saving.
  •   Keep the learning progression very clear:

                                     Page 212 of 420
        HR Balance Scorecard → Strategic Performance Metrics → Perspective Feedback
        → Workforce Performance Metrics → Workforce Performance Feedback → HR
        Coach / Team Reflection

    •   The Workforce Brief should answer three increasingly deeper questions:

        1. How did we perform?
        2. What happened within the workforce and organization?
        3. What should we examine and learn before making the next round of decisions?


One Note I Would Modify in Your Existing List
You currently say:

“Provide approximately 1,000–2,000 characters for teams to explain their HR decisions, interpret
their results, and reflect on what they learned before progressing to the next round.”

I would change that slightly to:

Add a Team Reflection section beside the HR Coach. Provide approximately 1,000–2,000
characters for teams to explain their HR decisions, interpret their results, and reflect on
what they learned. Save the reflection by Team + Round so teams can revisit their thinking
alongside each historical Workforce Brief. Reflection should support learning but should
not affect the simulation score.

Balance Sheet Financial Report – Design
& Programming Notes
 •   Add “Balance Sheet” as a dedicated financial report within the Reports area.
     The top financial-report navigation should remain consistent across all report
     pages: The Workforce Brief | Balance Sheet | Profit & Loss Statement | Cash
     Flow Statement | Financial Ratio Reports.
 •   Maintain the same visual design language as The Workforce Brief, including
     typography, white/gray background, blue primary headings, rectangular
     information cards, category-specific accent colors, left-side round navigation, and
     consistent spacing.
 •   Create a round-selection menu on the left side titled Financial Reports –
     Select a round to view results. Students should be able to select Balance Sheet –
     Round 1, Round 2, Round 3, etc., and return to previous rounds at any time.
 •   Display the report title as “BALANCE SHEET – ROUND X” with the
     applicable simulation date immediately underneath. Include a Download PDF
     Report function.
 •   Preserve historical financial data by round. Once a round is processed, that
     round's Balance Sheet should remain available so students can compare the
     organization's financial position over time.
 •   Add four Financial Snapshot cards across the top: Total Assets, Total
     Liabilities, Total Equity, and Working Capital. Each card should show the
     current-round value, prior-round value, and percentage/dollar change where
     appropriate.
 •   Use directional indicators carefully. A change should not automatically be
     coded green simply because it increased or red because it decreased. For example,
     increasing liabilities may be unfavorable while increasing cash may be favorable.
     The financial logic should determine the visual indicator.
 •   Organize the formal Balance Sheet into two major panels: Assets on the left
     and Liabilities & Equity on the right.
 •   Assets should initially include: Current Assets—Cash & Cash Equivalents,
     Accounts Receivable, Other Current Assets; Long-Term Assets—Property, Plant
     & Equipment, HR Technology & Systems, and Other Long-Term Assets.
 •   Liabilities should initially include: Current Liabilities—Accounts Payable,
     Accrued Compensation & Benefits, Other Current Liabilities; Long-Term
     Liabilities—Long-Term Debt and Other Long-Term Obligations.
 •   Shareholders' Equity should initially include: Contributed Capital and
     Retained Earnings.



                                  Page 215 of 420
•   Every financial line should display Current Round and Prior Round values
    so students can see changes rather than evaluating financial information in
    isolation.
•   Automatically calculate all subtotals and totals: Total Current Assets, Total
    Long-Term Assets, Total Assets, Total Current Liabilities, Total Long-Term
    Liabilities, Total Liabilities, Total Equity, and Total Liabilities & Equity.
•   The accounting equation must always reconcile: Total Assets = Total
    Liabilities + Total Equity. This should also become a validation check in the
    engine. If the two sides do not reconcile, the system should flag the calculation for
    administrative/diagnostic review rather than displaying an invalid student report.
•   Calculate Working Capital as Current Assets minus Current Liabilities. This
    becomes one of the four top-level financial snapshot measures.
•   Add a “Financial Health Indicators” section beneath the Balance Sheet
    containing Current Ratio, Debt-to-Assets, Debt-to-Equity, and Cash Position.
    Show both current-round and prior-round values.
•   Financial Health Indicators should be calculated from the underlying
    Balance Sheet rather than entered independently. This prevents conflicting
    numbers across reports.
•   Add “Workforce Financial Impact (Supplemental Information)” beneath the
    financial indicators. This is intentionally separate from the formal Balance Sheet
    because these measures primarily represent workforce investments/costs rather
    than Balance Sheet accounts.
•   The initial Workforce Financial Impact measures should include: Total
    Compensation & Benefits, Recruitment Investment, Training & Development
    Investment, HR Technology Investment, Turnover Cost, and Total Workforce
    Investment.
•   Total Workforce Investment should aggregate the applicable workforce
    expenditures generated by student HR decisions. Amit should use the existing
    engine variables as the source rather than creating duplicate financial inputs.
•   HR Technology requires special treatment. The engine will eventually need to
    distinguish between technology spending treated as a current expense and
    technology/system investments that may be represented as an organizational asset.
    For the current iteration, we can use the existing engine assumption consistently
    and refine the accounting treatment later.
•   Do not add coaching, recommendations, or detailed explanations to the
    Balance Sheet. This page is intended to function as an authentic financial
    statement students must interpret. Instructional interpretation belongs in The
    Workforce Brief, Team Reflection, classroom discussion, and eventually the
    HR Coach.
•   A short informational statement at the bottom is appropriate: the Balance
    Sheet represents the organization's financial position at the end of the selected
    round and should be used to understand how organizational and workforce
    decisions are influencing financial health.
•   The Balance Sheet should pull from the same centralized engine variables
    used by The Workforce Brief. Revenue, workforce expenses, compensation,
    recruitment costs, training expenditures, technology investments, turnover costs,


                                  Page 216 of 420
       cash, debt, and other values should not be separately calculated by each report
       page.
   •   Begin thinking of the financial reports as interconnected statements rather
       than separate pages. Eventually, Profit & Loss → Retained Earnings/Equity
       → Balance Sheet, while cash-generating and cash-consuming activities will
       connect to the Cash Flow Statement. The Financial Ratio Report should
       calculate its ratios from these underlying statements.
   •   Professor/Admin diagnostic functionality should eventually allow us to see
       the underlying calculation behind each Balance Sheet line item. This is
       important because I will need to test the structural integrity of the financial engine
       before we expose it to external Sandbox users.
   •   Do not overbuild the accounting engine in this iteration. The immediate goal is
       to establish the financial reporting structure, connect it to existing simulation
       variables, and create enough transparency for Dr. Cooper to test whether HR
       decisions are producing reasonable organizational financial consequences.
   •   Overall learning objective: students are not being trained to become
       accountants. The Balance Sheet exists so future HR professionals learn to read
       organizational financial information, recognize changes in financial health, and
       understand that HR decisions operate within the broader financial system of the
       organization.

Important engine relationship for Amit

The financial reports should not become four independent calculation engines. They
should be four different views of one underlying organizational financial model. A
change generated by an HR decision should flow through the engine once and then
appear consistently wherever that financial consequence belongs across The Workforce
Brief, Balance Sheet, Profit & Loss Statement, Cash Flow Statement, and Financial Ratio
Reports.

Additional Design & Programming Notes for Amit
   •   Use the approved Balance Sheet image as the primary visual design reference.

   •   The Balance Sheet is a post-round, read-only financial report. Students do not enter,
       edit, or save any values on this page.

   •   Only display a Balance Sheet after the applicable round has been processed and
       released. Do not generate a student-facing statement from unsubmitted or projected HR

•   For Round 1, where no prior simulation round exists, display a consistent baseline value

    Students should understand that the numbers represent their simulated organization rather

•   Maintain consistent financial terminology across all reports. For example, if the Balance

    Reports & HR Analytics.

    Investment, Training Investment, or Turnover Cost as Balance Sheet accounts.

•   Where the same workforce expenditure appears on the Profit & Loss Statement or Cash
    Flow Statement, it must originate from the same transaction/value, not a separately
    generated number.

    •   Do not use the Balance Sheet to tell students whether the organization is financially
        “good” or “bad.” The report presents financial position; interpretation occurs elsewhere.

One additional engine principle I would add
Think of each HR-related financial consequence as a single transaction or engine event that
may affect multiple financial statements differently—not as separate numbers generated for
each report.

For example, conceptually:

Training Investment

→ creates an expense where appropriate
→ affects profit
→ affects cash
→ ultimately affects the Balance Sheet through cash/equity

rather than programming:

Training expense for P&L = calculation A
Training cash flow = calculation B
Training Balance Sheet impact = calculation C

That distinction will prevent significant reconciliation problems later.

Your existing HR Technology note is exactly right, but for Iteration #4 I would make the
instruction even clearer:

For Iteration #4, do not attempt sophisticated capitalization/depreciation logic for HR
Technology. Apply one documented accounting assumption consistently across the financial
model. We can refine capitalization, useful life, and depreciation treatment after the core
financial statements reconcile reliably.

Bottom line
I would keep your existing notes exactly as they are and append these additions. You do not
need another 30–40-point rewrite.

Your Balance Sheet instructions already establish the most important architecture:

HR Decisions → One Financial Model → Finalized Round Data → Balance Sheet / P&L /
Cash Flow / Ratios

rather than:

HR Decisions → Four separately programmed reports.

That architecture is more important than any individual Balance Sheet design choice because it is
what will ultimately allow you to test whether the entire financial side of Real HR Simulation
actually reconciles.

Initial Calculation Logic

Amit should connect these directly to the engine rather than entering them manually:

   •   Total Revenue = organizational revenue generated for the round
   •   Total Operating Expenses = sum of all applicable operating expenses
   •   Operating Income = Total Revenue − Total Operating Expenses
   •   Net Profit = Operating Income − Interest and Other Applicable Expenses
   •   Operating Margin = Operating Income ÷ Total Revenue × 100
   •   Net Profit Margin = Net Profit ÷ Total Revenue × 100
   •   Revenue per Employee = Total Revenue ÷ Headcount
   •   Workforce Cost % of Revenue = Total Workforce Costs ÷ Total Revenue ×
       100

Connection to HR Decisions

   •   The P&L should help students see that HR decisions have financial
       consequences, but the relationship should not be oversimplified into “spending
       less is better.”
   •   Increased Recruitment spending may increase current expenses while improving
       future Hiring Quality, staffing capacity, and productivity.
   •   Training investment may reduce current-period profit while improving future
       skills, productivity, succession capability, and organizational performance.


                                    Page 224 of 420
   •   Compensation decisions increase workforce costs but may influence Engagement,
       Satisfaction, Retention, Hiring Quality, and Productivity.
   •   Turnover should create a visible financial consequence through Turnover Cost.
   •   HR Technology expenditures should connect to the appropriate existing HR
       Technology decisions and engine variables.
   •   These relationships should preserve the simulation's carry-forward logic. Some
       HR investments may have an immediate financial cost but produce benefits in
       later rounds.

Relationship to the Balance Sheet

   •   The P&L and Balance Sheet cannot operate as independent financial models.
   •   Net Profit/Loss generated by the P&L should eventually flow into Retained
       Earnings on the Balance Sheet.
   •   Expenses that consume cash should eventually connect to the Cash Flow
       Statement.
   •   Revenue, expenses, profit, assets, liabilities, and equity must therefore originate
       from a common financial model.
   •   We can refine the complete accounting relationships in later iterations, but Amit
       should structure the database/engine now so these reports can eventually connect
       without rebuilding them.

Relationship to The Workforce Brief

   •   Revenue and Profit shown in The Workforce Brief must exactly match the
       corresponding P&L values for the same round.
   •   Headcount used to calculate Revenue per Employee must be the same Headcount
       displayed in The Workforce Brief.
   •   Workforce costs must come from the same HR decision variables used to
       calculate HR metrics and business outcomes.
   •   Avoid duplicate formulas across report pages. The report pages should display
       engine results, not independently recreate them.

Student Learning Design

   •   Do not add HR Coach, Perspective Feedback, Metric Feedback, or Team
       Reflection to the P&L page.
   •   The P&L is an evidence/reporting document.
   •   Students should examine the financial results and determine what they mean.
   •   Interpretation should occur through The Workforce Brief, Team Reflection,
       classroom debrief, professor guidance, and eventually the HR Coach.
   •   Include only a short informational statement at the bottom explaining that the
       P&L summarizes organizational financial performance during the round and can
       be used to examine how workforce investments and decisions are influencing
       profitability.


                                    Page 225 of 420
Testing Requirements

   •   Professor/Admin testing should eventually allow Dr. Cooper to inspect the
       underlying variables used to generate each P&L line.
   •   During Sandbox testing, verify that:
          o Revenue matches The Workforce Brief.
          o Profit matches The Workforce Brief.
          o Workforce expenditures match student decisions.
          o Current and prior round comparisons are accurate.
          o Percentage changes calculate correctly.
          o Revenue per Employee uses the correct Headcount.
          o All expense categories reconcile to Total Operating Expenses.
          o Operating Income/Loss calculates correctly.
          o Net Profit/Loss calculates correctly.
          o Historical rounds remain unchanged after subsequent rounds are
              processed.

Important Programming Principle for Amit:
The Profit & Loss Statement is not another scoring system. It is a financial reporting
view of outcomes already produced by the Real HR Simulation engine. HR decisions
should enter the engine once, create workforce and organizational consequences, and
then those same results should populate The Workforce Brief, P&L, Balance Sheet, Cash
Flow Statement, and Financial Ratio Reports consistently.

Overall learning objective: The purpose is not to teach students accounting mechanics.
It is to help future HR professionals develop business and financial acumen by seeing
how recruitment, compensation, training, turnover, technology, and other workforce
decisions affect organizational costs, revenue, margins, and profitability over multiple
simulation rounds.

One accounting point I would clarify

Your expense architecture is very good, but I would give Amit one explicit warning about double
counting.

You have formal P&L expenses such as:

   •   Compensation & Wages
   •   Benefits
   •   Recruitment & Onboarding
   •   Training & Development
   •   Performance Management
   •   Employee Relations
   •   HR Technology
   •   Turnover Costs

and then many of those appear again under Workforce Cost Analysis.

I would add:


                                       Page 227 of 420
Important: Workforce Cost Analysis is supplemental categorization of expenses already
recognized in the financial model. These amounts should not be added again when
calculating Total Operating Expenses. The section provides an HR-focused analytical view
of the same underlying workforce costs.

That is worth stating explicitly because it is an easy programming mistake.

I would also clarify “Total Workforce Costs”

Amit should know exactly what this represents conceptually:

Total Workforce Costs should aggregate only the workforce-related expense categories
defined for the simulation. It should not automatically equal Total Operating Expenses
because Other Operating Expenses and other non-workforce costs may exist outside the
HR/workforce cost structure.

So conceptually:

Total Workforce Costs

    •   Other Non-Workforce Operating Expenses
        = Total Operating Expenses

assuming all operating expenses are captured within those two classifications.

That distinction will make the later financial ratios much cleaner.

One important timing issue: expenses vs. investments

You already address carry-forward logic well. I would add:

Do not assume that every HR decision produces its entire financial consequence in the
round in which the decision is made. The financial model should distinguish the timing of
the expenditure from the timing of workforce/organizational benefits where the simulation's
carry-forward logic requires it.

For example:

Round 1 Training Investment
→ Round 1 financial expense/cash consequence
→ potential Round 1 and/or later capability/productivity consequences

This preserves the distinction between financial recognition and simulation outcome timing.

Strengthen the Admin diagnostic slightly

Your testing section is already excellent. Since you are building the financial model now, I would
add three cross-statement checks for the Professor/Admin view:

                                         Page 228 of 420
    •   P&L Net Profit = Workforce Brief Profit ✓
    •   P&L Net Profit → appropriate Retained Earnings/Equity flow ✓
    •   Cash-affecting P&L items → Cash Flow Statement ✓

The Admin diagnostic could eventually show a simple Reconciled / Not Reconciled status.

Students never need to see this.

One terminology decision

You currently use both Net Profit (Loss) and later Net Profit/Loss. I would standardize the
formal statement terminology as:

Operating Income (Loss)
Net Profit (Loss)

Then use Profit as the shorter KPI terminology in The Workforce Brief if that is what you have
already established there.

That avoids unnecessary terminology drift.

I would leave everything else alone

In particular, these sections are already strong and do not need expansion:

Connection to HR Decisions — very good because it explicitly prevents students from
concluding that lower HR spending automatically means better performance.

Relationship to the Balance Sheet — exactly the architectural direction Amit needs.

Relationship to The Workforce Brief — establishes the one-source principle.

Student Learning Design — correctly keeps coaching and interpretation off the formal financial
statement.

Testing Requirements — unusually important at this stage and already detailed enough.

Important Programming Principle for Amit — keep this exactly as written.

I would therefore append perhaps 12–15 supplemental bullets, especially the double-counting,
Round 1 baseline, zero-denominator, read-only, and finalized-round rules. Otherwise, I would
consider the P&L instructions complete for Iteration #4.

The most important additional sentence for Amit is probably:

The formal P&L, Workforce Cost Analysis, Profitability Indicators, The Workforce Brief,
and eventually the other financial statements are different views of the same finalized

Financial Snapshot

   •   Display four KPI cards across the top: Operating Cash Flow, Investing Cash

       percentage/dollar change where appropriate.
   •   Keep this section simple. We do not need advanced cash-flow measures at this
       stage.

Cash Flow Statement

   •   Organize the formal statement into only three major sections: Cash from
       Operating Activities, Cash from Investing Activities, and Cash from
       Financing Activities.
   •   Display columns for Current Round, Prior Round, Change, and Change % to
       remain consistent with the P&L design.
   •   Under Cash from Operating Activities, initially include:
          o Cash Received from Operations
          o Compensation & Benefits Paid
          o Recruitment & Onboarding
          o Training & Development
          o Other Operating Costs
          o Net Cash from Operating Activities
   •   Under Cash from Investing Activities, initially include:

                                    Page 232 of 420
          o    HR Technology & Systems
          o    Other Organizational Investments
          o    Net Cash from Investing Activities
   •   Under Cash from Financing Activities, initially include:
           o Debt Borrowed / (Repaid)
           o Net Cash from Financing Activities
   •   Do not add unnecessary financing categories at this stage. We can expand the
       financial model in later iterations if the learning design requires it.
   •   At the bottom of the statement, prominently display:
           o Net Change in Cash
           o Beginning Cash Balance
           o Ending Cash Balance

Core Calculation Logic

   •   Net Operating Cash Flow = Cash Received from Operations − Operating
       Cash Payments
   •   Net Investing Cash Flow = Cash Received from Investments − Cash Used for
       Investments
   •   Net Financing Cash Flow = Financing Cash Received − Financing Cash
       Paid/Repaid
   •   Net Change in Cash = Operating Cash Flow + Investing Cash Flow +
       Financing Cash Flow
   •   Ending Cash Balance = Beginning Cash Balance + Net Change in Cash
   •   These calculations should come from centralized engine variables rather than
       being independently calculated or manually entered on the report page.

Workforce Cash Investments

   •   Add a supplemental section beneath the statement titled “WORKFORCE CASH
       INVESTMENTS (SUPPLEMENTAL INFORMATION).”
   •   Use four visual cards consistent with the approved image:
          o Compensation & Benefits
          o Recruitment & Onboarding
          o Training & Development
          o HR Technology & Systems
   •   Display the amount invested and the percentage of applicable cash outflow.
   •   This section is intended to make the HR connection visible without making the
       formal Cash Flow Statement overly complicated.
   •   Do not repeat every workforce expense from the P&L. The Cash Flow Statement
       should remain a cash report, not become a second P&L.

Relationship to Student Decisions

   •   Compensation and benefits decisions should affect cash paid for Compensation &
       Benefits.

                                    Page 233 of 420
   •   Recruitment decisions should affect Recruitment & Onboarding cash outflows.
   •   Training decisions should affect Training & Development cash outflows.
   •   Applicable HR technology decisions should affect HR Technology & Systems
       cash outflows.
   •   These values should be pulled from the same underlying student decisions and
       engine variables used throughout the simulation.
   •   Students should therefore be able to see that workforce investments require actual
       organizational resources, even when those investments may produce benefits in
       future rounds.

Critical Financial Integration

   •   Ending Cash Balance on the Cash Flow Statement must exactly equal Cash
       & Cash Equivalents on the Balance Sheet for the same round. This should
       become a formal engine validation rule.
   •   The Beginning Cash Balance for Round 2 should equal the Ending Cash
       Balance from Round 1, and the same carry-forward relationship should continue
       for subsequent rounds.
   •   Cash expenditures shown here should reconcile with the applicable
       expenses/investments generated elsewhere in the financial engine.
   •   Do not create separate cash-flow versions of Compensation, Recruitment,
       Training, or Technology spending. Use the same centralized engine values.

Important distinction for the simulation

   •   Profit and cash are not the same thing. Net Profit/Loss from the P&L should
       not simply be copied into Net Change in Cash.
   •   The P&L measures organizational financial performance during the round.
   •   The Cash Flow Statement measures actual movement of cash during the round.
   •   We do not need to teach students the full accounting reconciliation at this stage,
       but the engine should maintain this conceptual distinction.

Student Learning Design

   •   Do not add HR Coach, Team Reflection, or detailed explanatory feedback to this
       page.
   •   Include only the short informational statement shown in the approved design: the
       Cash Flow Statement shows how cash moved into and out of the organization
       during the round and helps students understand the cash impact of workforce
       investments and business decisions.
   •   The educational objective is for students to begin asking: Where did our cash
       go? What workforce investments consumed cash? Did those investments
       contribute to stronger organizational outcomes?
   •   Detailed interpretation remains within The Workforce Brief, Team Reflection,
       professor debrief, and eventually the HR Coach.



                                     Page 234 of 420
Testing Requirements

   •   During Sandbox validation, test that Ending Cash equals Balance Sheet Cash &
       Cash Equivalents.
   •   Verify that Beginning Cash correctly carries forward from the prior round.
   •   Verify that workforce cash expenditures correspond to the appropriate student
       decisions.
   •   Verify that the three cash-flow categories correctly calculate Net Change in Cash.
   •   Verify current-round/prior-round comparisons and percentage changes.
   •   Verify that historical Cash Flow Statements remain unchanged after later rounds
       are processed.
   •   Professor/Admin diagnostic tools should eventually allow Dr. Cooper to trace
       each displayed cash-flow amount back to its originating engine variable.

Important Programming Principle for Amit: The Cash Flow Statement should remain
intentionally simple. It is not a separate accounting engine and should not become
another version of the P&L. It is a reporting view showing how cash moved through the
organization based on the same underlying financial and HR decisions already processed
by the Real HR Simulation engine.

Overall learning objective: Help HR students develop business acumen by
understanding that HR decisions require cash, cash is a finite organizational resource,
and investments in people must ultimately be considered alongside the
organization's ability to generate and sustain financial resources over time.

Add One Important Note About Workforce Cash Investments

I would explicitly add:

Workforce Cash Investments is a supplemental analytical view of cash outflows already
represented in the formal Cash Flow Statement. These amounts must not be subtracted
again when calculating Net Change in Cash.

This is the Cash Flow equivalent of the double-counting safeguard we added to the P&L.

For example:

Training & Development = $80,000

appearing under Operating Activities and again under Workforce Cash Investments represents
one $80,000 cash event shown in two different views, not $160,000 of cash outflow.

Clarify the Percentage Used in Workforce Cash Investments

Your note currently says:

“Display the amount invested and the percentage of applicable cash outflow.”

I would define that more precisely for Amit. Otherwise, he will have to decide what denominator
to use.

I suggest:

For Iteration #4, calculate each Workforce Cash Investment percentage as that category's
cash outflow divided by Total Workforce Cash Outflows. Label the percentage clearly as
“% of Workforce Cash Outflows.” Do not use total organizational cash outflows unless we
intentionally change the definition later.

That gives students a much more meaningful HR-oriented comparison.




Add a Cash Timing Principle
This is probably the most important conceptual addition.


                                       Page 236 of 420
The P&L and Cash Flow Statement may recognize related events differently. Therefore add:

The financial engine should distinguish between when an expense/investment is recognized
and when cash actually moves. For Iteration #4, we can use simplified timing assumptions,
but those assumptions must be documented and applied consistently across rounds.

For example, we may initially assume:


Recruitment Expense Recognized in Round 2
Recruitment Cash Paid in Round 2

That is perfectly reasonable for Version 1.

But the architecture should not permanently assume:

Expense = Cash

because later we may introduce things such as accrued compensation, debt, receivables, or
technology assets.




Strengthen the Beginning/Ending Cash Rule
You already have this, but I would make it a formal state-transition requirement:

Ending Cash for Round X becomes Beginning Cash for Round X+1. Beginning Cash should
never be independently regenerated when a new round starts.

Conceptually:

Round 1 Ending Cash
↓
Round 2 Beginning Cash
↓

    •   Round 2 Net Cash Change
        ↓
        Round 2 Ending Cash
        ↓
        Round 3 Beginning Cash

That is one of the clearest examples of the carry-forward state logic we want throughout Real
HR Simulation.




                                        Page 237 of 420
Add Three Formal Reconciliation Checks
For the Professor/Admin diagnostic environment, I would add:

Cash Roll-Forward

Beginning Cash

    •   Net Change in Cash
        = Ending Cash ✓

Balance Sheet Reconciliation

Cash Flow Ending Cash
= Balance Sheet Cash & Cash Equivalents ✓

Round Carry-Forward

Prior Round Ending Cash
= Current Round Beginning Cash ✓

A fourth diagnostic could eventually be:

Workforce Cash Reconciliation

Workforce cash categories
= corresponding centralized financial-engine transactions ✓

These should be Admin-only diagnostic checks, not student-facing content.




One Point About Cash Received from Operations
I would clarify this because Amit could otherwise assume it equals Revenue.

Your statement currently has:

Cash Received from Operations

That is good, but add:

Cash Received from Operations should not automatically be assumed to equal P&L
Revenue. For the simplified Iteration #4 financial model, they may initially be equivalent if
we assume all operating revenue is collected during the round. If that assumption is used,
document it explicitly so Accounts Receivable/cash collection logic can be introduced later
without redesigning the reports.

                                       Page 238 of 420
This is important because you already have Accounts Receivable on the Balance Sheet.

If we have:

Revenue = $42.9M

and all $42.9M becomes cash immediately, then Accounts Receivable needs an explanation.
Otherwise the three statements can contradict one another.

For Iteration #4, a simplified assumption is completely reasonable—we just need it to be
intentional.




Same Issue With Accrued Compensation & Benefits
The Balance Sheet includes:

Accrued Compensation & Benefits

while Cash Flow includes:

Compensation & Benefits Paid

Therefore, conceptually:

Compensation Expense
does not always have to equal
Compensation Cash Paid

For Iteration #4, Amit can use a simplified assumption. But I would add:

Where Balance Sheet accounts such as Accounts Receivable or Accrued Compensation &
Benefits are active, the financial model should eventually distinguish recognized
revenue/expense from actual cash received/paid. Do not create that complexity unless
required for Iteration #4, but preserve the architecture for it.

That will save significant rebuilding later.




Directional Indicators Need the Same Caution
I would also add:

    •   Do not automatically treat positive Operating Cash Flow as green or negative
        Investing Cash Flow as red.
    •   Negative Investing Cash Flow may represent purposeful organizational investment.

                                          Page 239 of 420
    •   Positive Financing Cash Flow may mean the organization borrowed additional money,
        which is not automatically favorable.
    •   Therefore, use neutral directional indicators initially unless the interpretation is
        unambiguous.

This is particularly important on the Cash Flow Statement because positive/negative has much
less direct “good/bad” meaning than students may assume.




Keep HR Technology Simple
As with the Balance Sheet:

For Iteration #4, use one documented treatment for HR Technology cash expenditures. Do
not attempt advanced capitalization, depreciation, or multi-period cash-payment schedules
yet. The priority is that the same HR Technology transaction reconciles across the financial
model.




One Additional Student-Learning Detail
I would slightly strengthen your informational statement at the bottom:

The Cash Flow Statement shows how cash moved into and out of your organization during
the selected round. Use it to examine where organizational resources were generated, where
cash was committed, and how workforce investments affected the organization's ability to
maintain financial flexibility.

That reinforces the business-acumen objective without coaching students.




I Would Leave Everything Else Alone
Your existing notes already handle the most important areas exceptionally well:

    •   Three-section statement architecture
    •   Simple student-facing design
    •   Workforce cash connection
    •   Centralized engine variables
    •   Profit-versus-cash distinction
    •   Historical round preservation
    •   Balance Sheet reconciliation
    •   Carry-forward logic
    •   Sandbox testing
    •   No unnecessary coaching

                                        Page 240 of 420
    •   Iteration #4 scope boundary

The one concept I would especially highlight to Amit is:

A cash transaction should occur once in the financial engine and may then appear in
multiple reporting views. The formal Cash Flow Statement, Workforce Cash Investments,
P&L, Balance Sheet, The Workforce Brief, and Financial Ratios should never create
independent versions of the same underlying financial event.

So your financial architecture is now becoming very coherent:

Student HR Decision
→ Engine Transaction / Organizational Outcome
→ P&L recognition where applicable
→ Cash movement where applicable
→ Balance Sheet position
→ Financial Ratios
→ The Workforce Brief

That is the right architecture to preserve as Amit programs the financial side of Iteration #4.

Financial Ratio Report – Design &
Programming Notes
  •   Add “Financial Ratio Reports” as the final dedicated financial report within
      the Reports area. Maintain the common navigation: The Workforce Brief |
      Balance Sheet | Profit & Loss Statement | Cash Flow Statement | Financial
      Ratio Reports.
  •   Use the approved Financial Ratio Report image as the visual design
      reference. Maintain the same Real HR Simulation visual language used across all
      four financial reports.
  •   Display the report title as “FINANCIAL RATIO REPORT – ROUND X”
      with the subtitle “Financial Health & Workforce Efficiency.”
  •   Display the applicable simulation date immediately underneath the title.
  •   Maintain the Financial Reports round-selection menu on the left. Students
      should be able to access Financial Ratio Reports for all completed rounds and
      compare performance over time.
  •   Include the same Download PDF Report functionality used throughout the
      financial reporting area.
  •   Preserve historical ratio reports by round. Once a round has been processed,
      its ratios should remain unchanged.

Financial Ratio Dashboard

  •   The Financial Ratio Report should function primarily as a financial health
      dashboard rather than another detailed financial statement.
  •   Display six primary financial ratios in two rows of three rectangular KPI
      cards, consistent with the approved design.
  •   The six Version 1 measures should be:
          o Current Ratio
          o Debt-to-Assets Ratio
          o Debt-to-Equity Ratio
          o Operating Margin
          o Net Profit Margin
          o Revenue per Employee
  •   Each KPI card should include:
          o Current Round value
          o Prior Round value
          o Change from prior round
          o Directional indicator
          o Appropriate unit: percentage, ratio, or dollar value
  •   Retain the distinct icons and restrained accent colors shown in the approved
      design to make the six measures visually distinguishable.

Financial Ratio Summary
                                  Page 243 of 420
   •   Add a Financial Ratio Summary table immediately beneath the six KPI cards.
   •   Display columns for:
           o Ratio
           o Current Round
           o Prior Round
           o Change
           o Change % / percentage points
   •   The table should repeat the six primary ratios so students can quickly compare the
       current round against the previous round.
   •   Percentage-point changes should be identified as pp where appropriate rather than
       displayed as percentage growth.
   •   Maintain consistent rounding across rounds.

Ratio Calculation Logic

The six ratios should be calculated as follows:

   •   Current Ratio = Current Assets ÷ Current Liabilities
   •   Debt-to-Assets Ratio = Total Liabilities ÷ Total Assets × 100
   •   Debt-to-Equity Ratio = Total Liabilities ÷ Total Equity
   •   Operating Margin = Operating Income ÷ Total Revenue × 100
   •   Net Profit Margin = Net Profit ÷ Total Revenue × 100
   •   Revenue per Employee = Total Revenue ÷ Headcount
   •   These calculations should be performed automatically after each round is
       processed.
   •   No new student inputs are required to generate this report.
   •   All six ratios should derive from existing financial and workforce engine
       variables.

Critical Report Integration

   •   Current Assets and Current Liabilities must come directly from the Balance
       Sheet.
   •   Total Assets, Total Liabilities, and Total Equity must come directly from the
       Balance Sheet.
   •   Operating Income, Net Profit, and Total Revenue must come directly from
       the Profit & Loss Statement.
   •   Headcount must come from the same workforce variable used in The
       Workforce Brief.
   •   Do not recreate or independently calculate the underlying financial values within
       the Financial Ratio page.
   •   The Financial Ratio Report should calculate ratios from finalized engine outputs
       after the other financial statements have been generated.

This creates the intended flow:


                                     Page 244 of 420
Student HR Decisions → Simulation Engine → Organizational/Financial Outcomes
→ Financial Statements → Financial Ratios

Understanding the Ratios

   •   Retain the “Understanding the Ratios” section shown in the approved design.
   •   This section should provide only a short definition of each measure, not
       recommendations or coaching.
   •   Use the same icon/color associated with each ratio card for visual consistency.
   •   Initial definitions:
           o Current Ratio – Ability to meet short-term financial obligations by
                comparing current assets to current liabilities.
           o Debt-to-Assets Ratio – Percentage of organizational assets financed
                through debt.
           o Debt-to-Equity Ratio – Relationship between debt financing and
                shareholder equity.
           o Operating Margin – Operating income generated from each dollar of
                revenue.
           o Net Profit Margin – Final profit generated from each dollar of revenue
                after expenses.
           o Revenue per Employee – Revenue generated relative to the size of the
                workforce.
   •   These definitions should remain available each round because students may not
       have prior accounting or finance coursework.

Color and Directional Indicator Logic

   •   Do not automatically treat an increase as green and a decrease as red.
   •   The color must reflect whether the movement is generally favorable, unfavorable,
       or simply informational.
   •   For example:
           o Higher Revenue per Employee may generally be favorable.
           o A deteriorating Operating Margin is unfavorable.
           o A declining Current Ratio may indicate weakening liquidity.
           o Increasing leverage ratios require contextual interpretation and should not
              automatically be treated as positive.
   •   Where financial interpretation is ambiguous, use a neutral indicator rather than
       assigning a positive or negative judgment.
   •   This logic can be refined in later iterations as the simulation's financial model
       becomes more sophisticated.

Keep Version 1 Intentionally Limited

   •   Do not add additional financial ratios during this iteration.



                                    Page 245 of 420
   •   Specifically, we do not currently need ROA, ROE, Quick Ratio, Inventory
       Turnover, Receivables Turnover, Interest Coverage, EPS, P/E Ratio, or other
       traditional accounting measures.
   •   Additional ratios can be considered later if they support a specific industry,
       advanced course, or learning objective.
   •   The Version 1 objective is financial literacy and HR business acumen, not
       comprehensive financial analysis.

Student Learning Design

   •   Do not add HR Coach, Team Reflection, or decision recommendations to this
       report.
   •   The “Understanding the Ratios” section provides definitions only.
   •   Students should be expected to interpret what the movement in the ratios means
       for their organization.
   •   Deeper interpretation occurs through The Workforce Brief, Team Reflection,
       classroom debrief, professor guidance, and eventually the HR Coach.
   •   Include the informational statement shown in the approved design explaining that
       financial ratios provide a quick view of organizational financial health and
       efficiency and can be used to track trends and understand how decisions affect
       financial outcomes.

Testing Requirements

During Sandbox testing, validate that:

   •   Current Ratio exactly reconciles with Current Assets and Current Liabilities from
       the Balance Sheet.
   •   Debt-to-Assets reconciles with Total Liabilities and Total Assets.
   •   Debt-to-Equity reconciles with Total Liabilities and Total Equity.
   •   Operating Margin reconciles with Operating Income and Revenue from the P&L.
   •   Net Profit Margin reconciles with Net Profit and Revenue from the P&L.
   •   Revenue per Employee uses the same Revenue and Headcount displayed
       elsewhere in the simulation.
   •   Prior-round comparisons are accurate.
   •   Percentage versus percentage-point changes are calculated correctly.
   •   Historical reports remain unchanged when later rounds are processed.
   •   The Professor/Admin diagnostic environment eventually allows Dr. Cooper to
       trace each ratio back to its originating financial variables.

Important Programming Principle for Amit: The Financial Ratio Report should
introduce no new financial assumptions and no independent financial model. It is an
interpretation layer that mathematically summarizes data already generated by the
Balance Sheet, Profit & Loss Statement, and workforce engine. If an underlying financial
value changes, the corresponding ratio should update automatically.


                                    Page 246 of 420
Overall learning objective: The Financial Ratio Report completes the financial
reporting architecture by helping students move from simply seeing financial numbers to
beginning to interpret organizational financial health. Taken together, the financial
reports should answer four simple questions: Balance Sheet — Where are we
financially? P&L — Did we make money? Cash Flow — Where did our cash go?
Financial Ratios — What do these results tell us about the health and efficiency of
the organization?




Additional Design & Programming Notes for Amit
   •   The Financial Ratio Report should be post-round and read-only. Students
       should only see ratios after the applicable round has been processed and released.
   •   The selected round should control the entire page state. Selecting Round 4
       should load Round 4 ratios, Round 3 comparisons, summary-table values, and the
       corresponding definitions from the same finalized dataset.
   •   For Round 1, if no prior simulation round exists, use an established
       opening/baseline period where available. Otherwise display “—” or “Baseline”
       for prior-round/change fields rather than creating an artificial comparison.
   •   Once finalized, historical ratio reports should remain immutable unless a
       Professor/Admin intentionally reprocesses the underlying round.
   •   Do not silently substitute zero when a required denominator or source value is
       missing. If a required Balance Sheet, P&L, or Headcount value is unavailable,
       show N/A and create an Admin diagnostic flag.
   •   Define explicit zero-denominator behavior:
           o Current Liabilities = 0 → Current Ratio should display N/A or another
               defined convention.
           o Total Assets = 0 → Debt-to-Assets = N/A.
           o Total Equity = 0 → Debt-to-Equity = N/A.
           o Revenue = 0 → Operating Margin and Net Profit Margin = N/A.
           o Headcount = 0 → Revenue per Employee = N/A.
   •   Do not display Infinity, NaN, or divide-by-zero errors anywhere in the student
       interface.
   •   Use one consistent rounding convention throughout the financial architecture, for
       example:
           o Ratios such as Current Ratio and Debt-to-Equity: 2 decimals
           o Percentages/margins: 1 decimal or 2 decimals
           o Revenue per Employee: whole dollars or nearest $100
               The exact convention can be finalized later, but it should be centralized.
   •   The Download PDF Report should reproduce the finalized selected-round
       Financial Ratio Report and identify Company, Round, Industry, Strategy, and
       reporting date.
   •   The six KPI cards and the Financial Ratio Summary table must draw from the
       same calculated ratio objects, not independently recalculate the ratios for each
       display area.

One Important Clarification: Change vs. Change %
Your existing note is right to distinguish percentage-point changes. I would make the
implementation rule explicit.

Operating Margin
Round 1 = 8%
Round 2 = 10%

Display:

Change: +2.0 pp

not:

+25%

unless a separate relative-change field is intentionally provided.

The same applies to:

   •   Debt-to-Assets Ratio
   •   Net Profit Margin

For non-percentage ratios:

Current Ratio
1.40 → 1.60

display:

Change: +0.20

For dollar measures:

Revenue per Employee
$90,000 → $95,000

display:

Change: +$5,000 and, if desired, +5.6%.

That rule will make the summary table much easier to understand.

Clarify Favorable/Unfavorable Logic
I would go one step further than the current note:

Do not create a universal “good ratio” threshold during Iteration #4.

For example:

Current Ratio

   •   Higher is not infinitely better because excessive current assets may indicate
       inefficient resource use.

Debt-to-Equity

                                      Page 249 of 420
   •   Lower is not automatically optimal because some leverage may be strategically
       appropriate.

Revenue per Employee

   •   Higher may indicate productivity, but could also result from inadequate staffing.

Therefore, for Version 1 I would use three interpretation states:

Favorable Direction
only where the movement is clearly interpretable.

Caution
where the movement appears concerning.

Neutral / Context Required
where interpretation depends on strategy or conditions.

That aligns very well with the simulation's broader tradeoff philosophy.

One More Important Architecture Note
I would add:

Financial ratios should be calculated only after the underlying finalized financial
statements and workforce state have passed their validation checks. If the Balance
Sheet does not reconcile or the P&L contains a flagged calculation error, the
Financial Ratio Report should not present those ratios as valid student results.

That creates a logical processing sequence:

Round Processing

→ workforce outcomes
→ financial transactions
→ P&L
→ Cash Flow
→ Balance Sheet
→ financial reconciliation
→ Financial Ratios

rather than ratios being calculated from partially valid data.

I Would Keep the Six Ratios Exactly as They Are

Liquidity
Can the organization meet near-term obligations?

Leverage
How dependent is the organization on debt?

Profitability
Is the organization converting revenue into profit?

Workforce Efficiency
How much revenue is being generated relative to workforce size?

That is exactly enough financial literacy for this stage of the simulation.

                                      Page 252 of 420
I would append this to your existing Important Programming Principle:

The Financial Ratio Report is the final analytical layer of the financial reporting
architecture. It should only summarize validated values that already exist elsewhere.
If a ratio does not reconcile with its source financial statements, the problem should

Dr. Cooper’s Overall Comment to Amit
Amit, I really like this page. Team & Company now has a very clear purpose within the
simulation. It should become the organizational context hub—the place students return to
when they want to remind themselves what company they are managing, who is on their
team, what strategy they are pursuing, and what conditions surround the current round.

should be clearly identified as projections until the round is actually computed.

from the bottom of the navigation.
