simulation. We should not create duplicate company, team, budget, or engine data simply
for this page. The goal is to bring that information together in one intuitive organizational
view.

Finally, I want to keep this page intentionally simple. Team & Company should help
students understand the organization they are managing. It should not become another
decision page, analytics page, or project-management system.

Core design principle: The Dashboard tells students where they are in the simulation.
Team & Company reminds them who they are managing, who they are managing with,
and the organizational context within which their HR decisions must make sense.

Dr. Cooper's Overall Comment to Amit
Amit, I really like this page. My Team now has a simple and useful function within Team
& Company. Students can immediately see who is on their simulation team, how the
team is organized, and—most importantly—how collaboration and submission
permissions work.

I particularly like the optional team-role concept because it can help students organize
themselves without forcing us to build role-specific permissions. For Version 1, roles
such as Team Lead, Analyst, Strategist, Finance Lead, HR Lead, and Operations Lead


                                     Page 285 of 420
should simply help the team organize its work. They should not determine which
simulation pages or decisions a student can access.

The other important programming issue is that all students on the team must be working
from the same decision state. We are simulating one organization making one set of HR
decisions per round. Changes made by one team member therefore need to update the
team's shared decisions rather than creating individual versions.

I would also avoid building internal messaging simply because it appears in the design
image. If we are not ready to support that function, replace Team Communication with a
simple Team Collaboration panel that directs students to discuss their strategy and review
their decisions together. I would rather have fewer functions that work well than
additional buttons that do nothing.

Finally, keep My Team intentionally simple. We are not trying to build a project-
management or collaboration platform. This page should provide team identity,
participation context, and permission clarity while allowing students to get back to the
actual simulation quickly.

Core design principle: Real HR Simulation models one organization making one shared
set of decisions. My Team should make it clear who is making those decisions together
and how the team's shared decision environment works—without becoming a simulation
within the simulation.




                                     Page 286 of 420
Absolutely. I would make this page the permanent strategic reference point for the
team. The strongest part of the approved design is that it does more than repeat
“Manufacturing / Focus.” It translates those conditions into context students can use
while still requiring them to exercise judgment.

Dr. Cooper's Overall Comment to Amit
Amit, I really like this page. The Industry & Strategy Brief now gives students a
permanent strategic reference point inside the simulation rather than asking them to
remember information they saw only during Getting Started.

The five Industry Overview cards are particularly useful because students can quickly
understand the environment they are operating in—industry outlook, workforce

                                     Page 301 of 420
conditions, technology change, cost pressure, and market demand. Over time, these also
give us an architecture for changing conditions across rounds without redesigning the
simulation.

I also like the Key HR Implications section because it begins translating business strategy
into HR thinking without giving students the answer. That distinction is very important.
We can tell students that skilled talent is difficult to retain or that a Focus strategy
requires deep capabilities, but we should not tell them exactly how much to spend or
which dropdown option to select.

Please also treat this as the authoritative Industry & Strategy Brief. Getting Started should
link students to this content rather than maintaining another independent version. This
gives us one source of information that students can revisit throughout the simulation.

Finally, this page should eventually be configuration-driven. Industry, strategy, economy,
workforce outlook, technology change, cost pressure, market demand, and round
conditions should be capable of changing without requiring us to redesign the page. For
Iteration #4, however, the priority is establishing the architecture and making the
information clear and useful.

Core Design Principle

The Industry & Strategy Brief should tell students what environment they are managing
in and what strategic issues deserve attention—but the students must still decide what
those conditions mean for their HR strategy.

Programming Note

round.

Dr. Cooper's Overall Comment to Amit
Amit, I really like this page. Instructor Information completes the Team & Company
section and gives students one clear location for instructor, course, and simulation contact
information. I want this page to remain intentionally simple and primarily read-only.

The most important programming principle is that the information should come from the
instructor profile, course configuration, and simulation configuration rather than being
manually duplicated on this page. If the course is MGMT 470, has eight rounds, and is
taught by Dr. Cooper, those values should be established during setup and simply
displayed here.

I also like the Communication & Support section because it reinforces where students
should go for different needs. Course questions go to the instructor, technical issues go to
Help Center, and instructional/reference material goes to Resources. That separation will
become increasingly important as the simulation grows.

We should not build additional functionality simply because a button appears in the
design. If we do not have an internal Announcements system, hide that function. Email
Instructor can simply use the instructor's configured email. Likewise, View Course
Syllabus should only appear when a syllabus has actually been uploaded or linked.

Finally, this should not evolve into an LMS. We do not need grades, assignments, chat,
appointment scheduling, or course administration here. The purpose is much narrower:
give students the instructor and course context they need to participate successfully in the
simulation.

Core Design Principle

Team & Company tells students about the organization, their teammates, their strategic
environment, and their instructor. Instructor Information completes that architecture by
making support visible and accessible without distracting students from the simulation
itself.

   •   Why values may change

Decision Impact Preview ≠ Final Round Result

   •   Previous-round state

   •   Other HR decisions

   •   Final engine computation

35%.

The second version gives away the simulation logic.

40. Important Content Management Principle
Wherever possible, maintain one authoritative definition.

For example:

If Cost per Hire is defined in the Metric Glossary, The Workforce Brief tooltip and
Resources page should reference that same definition.

Do not independently write:

   •   one definition for Reports,

   •   another for Resources,

   •   another for Workforce Brief.

This prevents terminology from drifting as the simulation evolves.

Dr. Cooper’s Overall Comment to Amit
Amit, I really like this page. Resources now has a clear instructional purpose in the
simulation. It brings together the learning scaffolding we have already developed—the
seven HR Learning Guides, simulation explanations, metric definitions, and course
resources—without interrupting the decision-making environment.

The most important design principle is that Resources should help students understand
what they are looking at, but it should never tell them what decision to make. For example,
we can explain what turnover means, why Compensation Ratio matters, or how an industry
suggested range should be interpreted. We should not tell students which input to select to
maximize those outcomes.

Please also reuse content wherever possible rather than creating duplicate pages. The seven
HR Decision Learning Guides should be the same Pre-Simulation Learning pages we already
designed. Likewise, metric definitions should come from one central Metric Dictionary so
the definition students see in Resources is consistent with the terminology used in The
Workforce Brief and Reports & HR Analytics.

I also like separating the page into four clear areas: HR Decision Learning Guides,
Simulation Reference Center, HR Metrics Reference, and Downloads & Course Resources.
That is enough structure for Version 1 and gives us room to expand later without making
the page feel busy.

Finally, Resources should remain separate from Help Center. Resources helps the student
understand HR and the simulation concepts. Help Center helps the student use the software.
Keeping that distinction clear will make the entire navigation architecture much easier for
students to understand.

Core Design Principle

Resources should make students more informed decision-makers—not make the decisions
for them. The simulation provides the context and knowledge; students still have to
interpret the information, evaluate the tradeoffs, and exercise strategic HR judgment.

20. Strategic Tradeoffs Are Particularly Important
This should be one of the most valuable additions to the Learning Guides.

Example for Recruitment:

Selection Rigor

Higher rigor may strengthen candidate assessment but may also require more time and
resources.

Example for Training:

Training Investment

Greater investment can build capability, but HR resources committed here are
unavailable for other priorities.

Example for Compensation:

Market Position

                                      Page 351 of 420
Higher compensation can strengthen attraction and retention but increases organizational
labor costs.

Notice that none of these statements tells students:

Select High.

They explain the tradeoff.

That is exactly what we want.



21. Connections to Other HR Decisions
Every Learning Guide should explicitly show that HR decisions do not operate
independently.

Example:

Compensation & Benefits
Can we attract the talent we are targeting?

Training & Development
Are we hiring capability or developing it internally?

DEI Initiatives
How broad and inclusive are our talent pipelines?

Org Design & Change
What roles and capabilities does the organization actually need?

This reinforces systems thinking.

30. Progressive Learning Architecture
This is the larger instructional architecture I want Amit to understand:

Stage 1 – Before the Simulation

Pre-Simulation Learning Page

Purpose:

What is this HR area and why does it matter?

↓

Stage 2 – During Decision-Making

HR Decision Learning Guide

Purpose:

What concepts, metrics, connections, and tradeoffs should I understand?

↓

Stage 3 – Make the Decision

HR Decision Page

Purpose:

What will our organization choose?

                                      Page 356 of 420
↓

Stage 4 – Review

Review & Submit

Purpose:

Do our individual decisions form a coherent HR strategy?

↓

Stage 5 – Results

The Workforce Brief

Purpose:

What happened?

↓

Stage 6 – Reflection

HR Coaching + Team Reflection

Purpose:

What did we learn and what should we reconsider next round?

That is becoming the core instructional cycle of Real HR Simulation.

    •   AI tutor
    •   Personalized recommendations
    •   “Best Decision” indicators
    •   Decision optimization
    •   Exact scoring formulas
    •   Engine multipliers
    •   Correct-answer explanations

                                    Page 357 of 420
   •   Long textbook chapters
   •   Required quizzes
   •   Learning-guide scores
   •   Completion grades
   •   Complex filtering
   •   External internet resources

The Learning Guides should remain concise, contextual, strategic reference tools.



Dr. Cooper's Overall Comment to Amit
Amit, I really like this page. The HR Decision Learning Guides now give us the deeper
instructional layer that was missing between the Pre-Simulation Learning pages and the
actual HR Decision pages. The Pre-Simulation pages introduce students to each HR area;
these Learning Guides help them think more deeply once they are actively managing the
organization.

The most important difference is that the Learning Guides should add concepts, metrics,
strategic tradeoffs, connections to other HR areas, industry and strategy considerations,
what students should watch in their results, and reflection questions. They should be
approximately 30–40% deeper than the Pre-Simulation pages without becoming textbook
chapters.

Please also be careful with the Key Metrics shown in the design. We should only display
metrics that actually exist in the validated simulation engine or reporting architecture. We
should not invent a metric simply because we need three items on every card. Where a
module has more useful concepts than validated metrics, label the section Key Concepts
instead.

I particularly want the Strategic Tradeoffs and Connections sections to become important
features of these guides. Students need to understand that HR decisions involve
competing priorities and that Recruitment, Performance, Training, Employee Relations,
Compensation, Org Design, and DEI do not operate independently. At the same time, we
must stop short of telling students what decision to make.

Ultimately, the Learning Guides should support a very simple learning process: Learn,
Consider, Decide, Review, Observe Results, and Reflect. That process should
increasingly become visible throughout the Real HR Simulation experience.

Core Design Principle

The Pre-Simulation pages teach students what they are about to encounter. The HR
Decision Learning Guides teach them how to think more deeply about what they are
encountering. Neither tells them what decision to make.

7. Understanding Industry Guidance & Suggested
Ranges

Understanding Industry Guidance & Suggested Ranges

Description:

them strategically.

Students should learn:

   •     What industry guidance represents
   •     Why industries have different reference ranges
   •     What it means to be inside or outside a range
   •     How ranges support comparison and judgment



8. Suggested Ranges Are Not Targets
This needs to be stated very clearly in the full reference.

Suggested Range ≠ Correct Answer

For example:

Manufacturing Suggested Range: 65%–75%

means:

Organizations operating in this environment commonly invest within this general range.

                                      Page 363 of 420
It does not mean:

70% is the optimal decision.

Students may intentionally operate outside a suggested range because of:

   •     Strategy
   •     Current organizational conditions
   •     Other HR investments
   •     Previous-round outcomes
   •     Team priorities

That is part of strategic decision-making.



9. Warnings Should Be Explained Here
This is also the appropriate place to explain warnings.

Example:

Below Suggested Range

means:

Your current investment is below the industry's reference range. Review the
decision and determine whether the difference is strategically justified.

It should not mean:

Increase your investment.

This distinction should remain consistent throughout all HR Decision pages.



10. Understanding Decision Impact Previews
Display:

Understanding Decision Impact Previews

Description:


                                     Page 364 of 420
Learn how previews are generated and how to use them to anticipate potential
outcomes.

Students should learn:

   •      What the preview shows
   •      What the preview does not show
   •      Why previews are estimates
   •      Why previews can change as decisions change
   •      How to use previews to evaluate strategic tradeoffs



11. Projected vs. Actual Outcomes
This should be one of the most important explanations in the reference.

Projected Outcome

An estimate based on the team's current decision state.

versus:

Actual Outcome

The result produced after the round is submitted and the simulation engine
processes the complete organizational state.

Explain that actual results can reflect:

   •      Multiple HR decisions
   •      Industry
   •      Strategy
   •      Economy
   •      Previous organizational state
   •      Round conditions

Do not expose the actual mathematical relationships.



12. Impact Previews Should Not Become Guarantees
Use language such as:


                                          Page 365 of 420
Decision Impact Previews help you anticipate possible consequences. They should
support your judgment—not replace it.

Students should understand that:

+3.4 Retention Impact

does not necessarily mean the final retention metric will improve by exactly 3.4 points.

This distinction needs to be consistent throughout the simulation.



13. Understanding the HR Budget
Display:

Understanding the HR Budget

Description:

Understand how your discretionary HR budget works and how it connects to the
broader organization.

Students should learn:

   •      What the HR budget includes
   •      What the HR budget does not include
   •      How HR allocations affect available resources
   •      Why allocation decisions involve tradeoffs



14. Discretionary HR Budget vs. Organizational Costs
This should explain one of the most important financial distinctions in the simulation.

Discretionary HR Budget

Resources students allocate through the seven HR Decision modules.

versus:

Organizational Financial Costs


                                      Page 366 of 420
Broader expenses such as:

   •   Base payroll
   •   Benefits
   •   Bonus costs
   •   Operating expenses
   •   Other organizational costs

Therefore:

$500,000 HR Budget

does not mean:

Total organizational HR-related expenses are $500,000.

This should help students understand the financial pages we recently designed.



15. Budget Tradeoffs
Explain that the budget is intentionally constrained.

If a team allocates more resources to:

Training & Development

those resources may not be available for:

Recruitment, Employee Relations, DEI, Compensation, or other HR priorities.

Again, do not tell students what allocation is optimal.

The reference explains the tradeoff.



16. Understanding The Workforce Brief
Display:

Understanding The Workforce Brief

Description:

                                       Page 367 of 420
Learn how to read and use The Workforce Brief to evaluate results and guide future
decisions.

Students should learn:

   •   What is included in The Workforce Brief
   •   How to interpret KPIs and summaries
   •   How to use Coaching Insights
   •   How to reflect and plan with the team



17. Workforce Brief Reference Structure
The full reference should explain the major sections:

Strategic Performance Metrics

The highest-level organizational results.

Perspective Summaries

Financial, Employee, Internal Process, and Learning & Growth.

Workforce Performance Metrics

Specific HR outcomes.

HR Coaching

Interpretive guidance that helps students think about the results.

Team Reflection

Questions designed to help teams discuss what happened and what they might reconsider.



18. Review & Submit vs. Workforce Brief
Include a very simple distinction:

Review & Submit

Before the round is processed
                                      Page 368 of 420
What are we about to do?

The Workforce Brief

What happened?

This will prevent students from confusing projected and actual results.



19. Understanding Financial Reports
Display:

Understanding Financial Reports

Description:

Learn how to read financial statements and key ratios to assess organizational
performance.

Students should learn about the four reports we approved:

   •   Balance Sheet
   •   Profit & Loss Statement
   •   Cash Flow Statement
   •   Financial Ratios

This should remain financial literacy for HR leaders, not an accounting course.



20. Balance Sheet Reference
Explain simply:

Balance Sheet

What does the organization own, what does it owe, and what is its financial
position?

Students should understand:


                                     Page 369 of 420
Assets = resources

Liabilities = obligations

Equity = residual organizational value

Keep the explanation practical and simulation-oriented.



21. Profit & Loss Reference
Explain:

Profit & Loss Statement

How much revenue did the organization generate, what did it cost to operate, and
was the organization profitable?

Focus attention on:

   •   Revenue
   •   Major costs
   •   HR-related expenses
   •   Operating profit
   •   Net profit

This helps students see that HR decisions ultimately have organizational financial
consequences.



22. Cash Flow Reference
Keep this very simple, as we previously decided.

Explain:

Cash Flow Statement

Where did cash come from, where did it go, and did the organization's cash position
increase or decrease?

Focus on:


                                     Page 370 of 420
   •   Operating activities
   •   Investing activities
   •   Financing activities
   •   Ending cash balance

No need for advanced accounting terminology.



23. Financial Ratios Reference
Explain the small group of ratios we approved.

The purpose should be:

Ratios help students interpret financial numbers rather than looking at dollar
amounts in isolation.

Explain:

   •   What each ratio measures
   •   Whether it relates to profitability, liquidity, efficiency, or HR investment
   •   Why an HR leader might care

Again, keep it simple.

27. Do Not Reveal Structural Logic
The Simulation Reference Center should never expose:

      •   Decision weights
      •   Engine formulas
      •   Normalization equations
      •   Industry multipliers
      •   Strategy multipliers
      •   State-transition formulas
      •   Threshold logic
      •   Exact scoring relationships
      •   Optimal decisions

This is important because students need enough transparency to understand the
simulation without being able to reverse-engineer it.

36. Iteration #4 Boundary
Do not add:

   •   Engine formulas
   •   Scoring weights
   •   Optimal decisions
   •   AI explanations
   •   Personalized recommendations
   •   Decision troubleshooting
   •   Technical support
   •   Detailed accounting instruction

   •   External websites
   •   Quizzes
   •   Reference completion tracking
   •   Reference scoring
   •   Complex search

For Iteration #4, six well-designed reference articles are sufficient.



Dr. Cooper’s Overall Comment to Amit
Amit, I really like this page. The Simulation Reference Center now gives students one
clear location to understand the major features and information they encounter
throughout Real HR Simulation—the HR Balance Scorecard, industry guidance,
Decision Impact Previews, HR budget, The Workforce Brief, and financial reports.

The most important distinction is that this page explains the simulation rather than
teaching the underlying HR discipline. The HR Decision Learning Guides help students
understand HR concepts and strategic tradeoffs. The Simulation Reference Center helps
students understand what the simulation is showing them and how they should interpret
it.

Please make one important change from the design image under the HR Balance
Scorecard. Rather than saying we will explain “how scores are calculated and
normalized,” use “how scores are structured and interpreted.” I do not want the Reference
Center exposing scoring formulas, engine weights, multipliers, or other structural logic.



                                      Page 378 of 420
Students should understand the meaning of the simulation without being able to reverse-
engineer it.

I also want these six references to become our authoritative explanations throughout the
simulation. For example, when students click “About These Estimates” on a Decision
Impact Preview, that should ultimately connect to the Decision Impact Preview reference
rather than us writing another independent explanation. The same principle should apply
to Suggested Ranges, HR Budget, The Workforce Brief, and Financial Reports.

Finally, keep these references practical and visual. Use examples that look like Real HR
Simulation rather than generic textbook examples. The purpose is to help students
understand what they are seeing so they can make better judgments—not to tell them
what decisions to make.

Core Design Principle

The HR Decision Learning Guides teach students how to think about HR. The Simulation
Reference Center teaches students how to understand Real HR Simulation. Together,
they provide the knowledge students need while preserving the judgment, uncertainty,
and strategic tradeoffs that make the simulation a learning experience.

14. Standard Metric Detail Structure
The right-side Metric Definition Guide in the approved image gives us a very good
architecture.

Each metric should contain:

Definition

What does this metric measure?


                                    Page 386 of 420
Why It Matters

Why should an HR leader care about it?

How It's Calculated

High-level calculation logic.

How to Interpret It

What generally does higher/lower mean?

What to Watch

What changes or patterns deserve attention?

Strategic Insight

What questions should the team consider when interpreting the result?

I would use this structure consistently across the entire Metric Dictionary.



15. “How It's Calculated” Requires Care
This is important.

We can provide standard HR formulas when they are part of normal HR literacy.

For example:

Turnover Rate

Employees Who Left ÷ Average Number of Employees × 100

or:

Cost per Hire

Total Recruiting Costs ÷ Number of Hires

That is educationally appropriate.

However, do not expose proprietary structural-engine calculations such as:

                                     Page 387 of 420
Turnover = Compensation Weight × 0.28 + Engagement × 0.31 + ...

That would reveal simulation logic.

So:

Standard HR metric formula = OK

Simulation engine outcome formula = Not shown



16. Distinguish Metric Formula From Engine Formula
This distinction should be explicitly maintained in the content architecture.

Example:

Training ROI

We can explain the standard conceptual calculation.

But we should not explain exactly how:

Training Investment + Program Mix + Economy + Industry + Prior State

produces the simulation's Training Effectiveness outcome.

Students need to understand the metric without reverse-engineering the simulation.



17. How to Interpret Higher and Lower Values
Where appropriate, explain directional interpretation.

For example:

Turnover Rate

Generally, lower turnover indicates greater workforce stability. However, extremely
low turnover is not automatically evidence of organizational effectiveness.

Employee Engagement


                                      Page 388 of 420
Higher values generally indicate stronger employee enthusiasm and commitment.

Cost per Hire

Lower cost is not automatically better if hiring quality declines.

This is especially important because it reinforces tradeoffs rather than optimization.



18. Avoid “Green = Good / Red = Bad”
Do not universally code:

Higher = Green

Lower = Red

Some metrics are inverse.

For example:

Turnover ↓ generally favorable.

Engagement ↑ generally favorable.

Cost per Hire ↓ may appear favorable, but could reflect underinvestment.

Use colors primarily for category organization unless the simulation has an explicit
interpretation rule.



19. What to Watch
This section should help students look for patterns.

Example:

Cost per Hire – What to Watch

Consider:

   •   Is Cost per Hire increasing across rounds?
   •   Is Hiring Quality also changing?

                                     Page 389 of 420
   •   Is Time to Fill improving or worsening?
   •   Are Recruitment investments producing the intended workforce outcomes?

Notice that this asks students to connect metrics rather than react to one number.



20. Strategic Insight
End each detailed metric page with a question.

Example:

Think Like an HR Leader

For Turnover:

Is our turnover level consistent with the workforce stability and capabilities our
strategy requires?

For Training ROI:

Are our training investments producing enough organizational value relative to the
resources committed?

For Compensation Ratio:

Are our compensation costs financially sustainable while still supporting our talent
strategy?

Questions—not answers.

The Metric Reference teaches students how to interpret them.

Dr. Cooper’s Overall Comment to Amit
Amit, I really like this page. The HR Metrics Reference should become the authoritative
dictionary for every HR metric used throughout Real HR Simulation. Students will
encounter these measures in Decision Impact Previews, The Workforce Brief, Reports &
HR Analytics, and other areas, so we need one consistent place where they can
understand exactly what each metric means.

Please be careful not to create metrics simply because they appear in the design image.
Performance Quality, Representation Index, Inclusion Index, Digital Capability, and even
Total Compensation need to be confirmed against the actual engine and reporting
architecture before we treat them as formal metrics. It is perfectly fine for one category to
contain two metrics and another to contain four. Engine validity is more important than
visual symmetry.

I also want each metric to follow the same learning architecture: Definition, Why It
Matters, How It's Calculated at a standard/high level, How to Interpret It, What to Watch,
Related Metrics, and a Strategic Insight question. We can teach standard HR formulas
such as Turnover Rate or Cost per Hire, but we should never expose the structural logic
showing exactly how student decisions generate simulation outcomes.

Most importantly, build this as one Metric Dictionary rather than a collection of
independent definitions. If Turnover Rate appears in The Workforce Brief, Reports, and

                                      Page 399 of 420
Resources, all three should draw from the same metric name, definition, unit, and
interpretation. That will become increasingly important as the simulation grows.

Core Design Principle

The HR Metrics Reference should not tell students whether they made the right decision.
It should give them the measurement literacy to understand what happened, connect
multiple outcomes, recognize patterns across rounds, and decide for themselves what
those results mean for their HR strategy.

8. Decision Worksheet Template

Decision Worksheet Template

Description:

Worksheet to help your team analyze options and document key decisions.

I like the spreadsheet format shown in the design.

Potential future worksheet columns could include:

HR Area | Current Decision | Proposed Decision | Rationale | Expected Outcome |
Tradeoff | Team Notes

However, this should remain an optional planning tool.

Students should not have to upload the worksheet back into the simulation unless an
instructor separately makes that a course requirement.




9. Team Reflection Template
Display:

Team Reflection Template

Description:

Structured reflection questions to help your team learn and improve each round.

This can complement the on-screen Team Reflection in The Workforce Brief.

The downloadable version could be useful when an instructor wants teams to submit a
formal reflection assignment.




10. Simulation Resources Are Platform-Level
These four resources should be controlled by the Real HR Simulation administrator rather
than individual instructors.

Conceptually:

Simulation Administration




                                     Page 405 of 420
→ Upload Standard Resource
→ Replace Resource
→ Version Resource
→ Publish Resource

↓

Available to all applicable courses.

Instructors should not accidentally overwrite the master Student Simulation Guide.




11. Course Resources
Create:

Course Resources

Provided by Your Instructor

Subtitle:

Additional materials specific to your course.

The approved design uses good examples:

    •     Assignment Instructions

    •     Course Syllabus

    •     Team Charter Template

    •     Instructor Presentation Slides

    •     Grading Rubrics

    •     Reflection Journal Prompts

These should be examples of instructor-configured resources, not six mandatory
documents.




12. Course Resources Must Be Dynamic
This is important for Amit.

Do not hard-code these six course documents.

One instructor might provide:

                                           Page 406 of 420
Course Syllabus
Simulation Assignment Instructions

Another might provide:

Team Contract
Board Presentation Instructions
Reflection Paper Rubric

Another might provide only:

Course Syllabus

The page should simply display whatever resources have been assigned to that
course/session.




13. Instructor Upload Capability
Eventually, the instructor/admin environment should allow:

Add Course Resource

with fields such as:

    •   Resource Name

    •   Description

    •   File

    •   Category

    •   Student Visibility

    •   Publish Date

Then:

Upload / Publish

For Iteration #4, we primarily need the student-facing architecture, but please structure the
data so instructor-uploaded resources can be supported later.




14. Resource Table Structure
I really like the approved table.


                                      Page 407 of 420
Maintain columns:

Resource | Description | Type | Size | Last Updated | Download

This gives students everything they need without making the table busy.

   •     open the document in-browser where supported, or

   •     download the document.

350 KB

etc.

Last Updated

Example:

May 15, 2026

This becomes especially useful as simulation guides evolve.

Students can quickly determine whether they have the most recent version.

The system should generate this from the resource metadata rather than instructors
manually typing the date.




19. Download Icon

Click:

↓

→ downloads the corresponding file.

Do not display the icon if no actual file has been uploaded.

Again, no inactive buttons.

29. Version Control – Keep Simple
We do not need sophisticated document versioning during Iteration #4.

At minimum, store:

   •     File name

   •     Display name

   •     Upload date

   •     Last updated date

   •     Uploaded by

   •     Resource category

   •     Active/inactive status

When a resource is replaced, students should see only the current active version.


                                     Page 413 of 420
We can build historical versioning later if necessary.




30. Course Isolation
This will become very important as Real HR Simulation scales.

Course resources should only be visible to students in the applicable course/session.

For example:

MGMT 470 – Fall 2026

resources should not automatically appear for:

MBA 570 – Spring 2027

unless the instructor intentionally assigns them there.

Simulation Resources can be global.

Course Resources must be session/course-specific.




31. Permissions
Students

Can:

   •   View

   •   Open

   •   Download

Cannot:

   •   Upload

   •   Delete

   •   Replace

   •   Rename

Instructor

Eventually can:


                                       Page 414 of 420
      •   Upload

      •   Edit description

      •   Replace

      •   Remove

      •   Publish/unpublish course resources

Simulation Administrator

Can manage standard Simulation Resources.

This separation should be built into the architecture.




32. Do Not Turn This Into Assignment Submission
This is a major boundary.

Downloads & Course Resources should not become:

      •   Submit Assignment

      •   Upload Student Work

      •   Grade Assignment

      •   Return Feedback

      •   Track Submission

      •   Calculate Grades

Those functions belong in the institution's LMS.

Real HR Simulation should complement systems such as Blackboard or Canvas—not
attempt to replace them.




33. No Student File Storage
Likewise, students should not need:

My Files

or:

Team Files

                                      Page 415 of 420
inside Real HR Simulation during Version 1.

Teams can use their institution's existing collaboration environment.

The purpose here is simply:

Instructor/Simulation Team → Student Resources

not:

Student → File Management Platform




34. Accessibility
Downloadable resources should eventually support accessibility expectations.

Where possible:

   •   Accessible PDFs

   •   Proper document headings

   •   Descriptive file names

   •   Readable tables

   •   Alternative text in instructional documents

This is primarily a content-development issue rather than a major Iteration #4
programming requirement, but the platform should not interfere with accessible files.




35. Resource Data Architecture
Conceptually:

Simulation Resource Library

→ Student Simulation Guide
→ Quick Start Checklist
→ Decision Worksheet
→ Team Reflection Template

and:

Course Resource Library



                                     Page 416 of 420
→ Course ID
→ Session ID
→ Instructor ID
→ Resource
→ Description
→ Type
→ Size
→ Last Updated
→ Visibility

↓

Downloads & Course Resources

This keeps global simulation materials separate from course-specific materials.




36. One Resource – Multiple Entry Points
This should become a broader design principle.

For example:

Course Syllabus

can appear in:

Resources
and Instructor Information.

Quick Start Checklist

can appear in:

Resources
and Getting Started.

Team Reflection Template

can appear in:

Resources
and The Workforce Brief.

Student Simulation Guide

can appear in:



                                     Page 417 of 420
Resources
and Help Center.

But each should point to the same underlying resource.

Do not maintain duplicate files.




37. Bottom Navigation
Retain:

← Back to Resources

and:

Back to Dashboard

No:

Save Now

No:

All changes auto-saved

Students are consuming resources, not modifying simulation state.




38. Iteration #4 Boundary
Do not add:

      •   Assignment submission

      •   Student file uploads

      •   Gradebook

      •   Instructor grading

      •   Peer review

      •   Team shared drives

      •   Document editing

      •   Comments on documents

      •   Document collaboration

                                    Page 418 of 420
   •   LMS synchronization

   •   Google Drive integration

   •   OneDrive integration

   •   Complex document versioning

   •   Required-resource completion tracking

   •   Resource-use scoring

For Iteration #4, we need a simple, clean resource library that works reliably.




Dr. Cooper’s Overall Comment to Amit
Amit, I really like this page. Downloads & Course Resources completes the Resources
section and gives students one clean location for the documents and templates they may
need throughout the simulation. The most important design feature is the separation
between Simulation Resources provided by the Real HR Simulation team and Course
Resources provided by the individual instructor.

Please build those as two different resource types. Simulation Resources should be
platform-level materials such as the Student Simulation Guide, Quick Start Checklist,
Decision Worksheet, and Team Reflection Template. Course Resources should be specific to
the course/session and should eventually be configurable by the instructor. The Assignment
Instructions, Syllabus, Team Charter, Presentation Slides, Rubrics, and Reflection Prompts
shown in the image are examples—not required resources for every course.

I also want us to follow the same one-source principle we have used elsewhere. If the
Course Syllabus appears here and under Instructor Information, both links should point to
the same file. If the Quick Start Checklist appears here and in Getting Started, it should be
the same resource. We should not maintain duplicate copies of documents throughout the
simulation.

Finally, please keep this function intentionally limited. We are providing resources to
students; we are not building another learning management system. Students should be
able to view and download materials, while instructors eventually manage their course
resources through the instructor environment. Assignment submission, grading, student file
storage, and document collaboration should remain outside Real HR Simulation.

Core Design Principle

Resources should now have four very clear instructional functions: HR Decision Learning
Guides help students understand HR decisions; the Simulation Reference Center helps them
understand the simulation; the HR Metrics Reference helps them understand what the

                                      Page 419 of 420
results measure; and Downloads & Course Resources gives them the documents and tools
they need to support their work.




                                   Page 420 of 420
